\hat{h}_{i}\coloneqq d_{i}\cdot(\hat{x}_{0,i}\oplus\hat{x}_{1,i})\in\{0,1\}
and

\hat{h}^{\prime}\coloneqq(\hat{h}_{N+1},\ldots,\hat{h}_{2N},\hat{h}_{1},\hat{h}_{2},\ldots,\hat{h}_{N})\in\{0,1\}^{2N}.

(3.4)

For
v\in\{0,1\}^{2N}
, we also define the state

\ket{\psi^{v}}\coloneqq\frac{1}{\sqrt{2^{N}}}\bigotimes_{i=1}^{N}(\sigma^{X})^{v_{i}}\otimes(\sigma^{X})^{v_{N+i}}(\ket{0}_{i}\ket{+}_{N+i}+\ket{1}_{i}\ket{-}_{N+i}),

(3.5)

which consists of
N
(locally-rotated) EPR pairs.

Then, the device applies
N
controlled-
\sigma^{Z}
gates between the
i
-th and
(N+i)
-th qubits of
\bigotimes_{i=1}^{2N}\ket{\alpha_{i}}
for all
i\in[N]
(note that the controlled-
\sigma^{Z}
gate is independent of which qubit is the control and which qubit is the target). The device has now prepared the
2N
-qubit state

\ket{\alpha}\coloneqq\begin{cases}\ket{\hat{b}_{1},\ldots,\hat{b}_{\theta-1}}\ket{(-)^{\hat{b}_{\theta+N}\oplus\hat{h}_{\theta}}}\ket{\hat{b}_{\theta+1},\ldots,\hat{b}_{2N}}&\text{if $\theta\in[2N]$, $\theta\leq N$},\\
\ket{\hat{b}_{1},\ldots,\hat{b}_{\theta-1}}\ket{(-)^{\hat{b}_{\theta-N}\oplus\hat{h}_{\theta}}}\ket{\hat{b}_{\theta+1},\ldots,\hat{b}_{2N}}&\text{if $\theta\in[2N]$, $\theta>N$},\\
\ket{\hat{b}_{1},\ldots,\hat{b}_{2N}}&\text{if $\theta=0$},\\
\ket{\psi^{\hat{h}^{\prime}}}&\text{if $\theta=\diamond$}.\end{cases}

(3.6)

In the “Hadamard” case, there is a third and final round where the verifier sends a bitstring
q\in\{0,1\}^{2N}
to the device. The device performs the following
q
-dependent measurements. For
i\in[2N]
, if
q_{i}=0
, measure the
i
th qubit of
\ket{\alpha}
in the computational basis, otherwise, measure the
i
th qubit of
\ket{\alpha}
in the Hadamard basis.
The device finally sends the outcome
u\in\{0,1\}^{2N}
of these measurements to the verifier. The expressions on the right-hand side of
Eq.

3.6
imply that the device passes the last checks made by the verifier with probability
\geq 1-2N\cdot{\mathrm{negl}(\lambda)}
. (The only way the device fails these last checks is if
d_{i}=0^{w}
for some
i\in[2N]
, which happens with probability at most
2N\cdot{\mathrm{negl}(\lambda)}
).

The “moreover” part of the theorem follows directly from the efficient function generation and the efficient decoding properties of ENTCFs (
(a)
and
(f)
).
∎

4
Soundness of self-testing protocol

In this section, we show that our self-testing protocol achieves
\mathrm{poly}(N,\epsilon)
soundness error. Unlike the proof of completeness in
Section

3
, we use the adaptive hardcore bit and injective invariance properties of ENTCFs to prove soundness in this section. Therefore, it is necessary for us to make the LWE hardness assumption throughout this section.

We start with
Section

4.1
where we mathematically model quantum devices. Essentially, a quantum device is a four-tuple
D=(S,M,\Pi,P)
where
S
is a set of states,
M
,
\Pi
, and
\{P_{q}\mid q\in\{0,1\}^{2N}\}
are the measurements
D
performs to obtain the
d
,
(b,x)
, and
u
given question
q
, respectively, as defined in our protocol (
Fig.

2
). We use
P_{q}
to define observables of the quantum device called
X_{q,i}
and
Z_{q,i}
for
i\in[2N]
that should act as
\sigma^{X}_{i}
and
\sigma^{Z}_{i}
, respectively. (Recall that
\sigma^{X}_{i}
is the Pauli
X
operator acting on qubit
i
and
\sigma^{Z}_{i}
is the Pauli
Z
operator acting on qubit
i
.) For different choices of
(\theta,q,i)\in([2N]\cup\{0,\diamond\})\times\{0,1\}^{2N}\times[2N]
, our goal is to characterize the actions of the observables
X_{q,i}
and
Z_{q,i}
on the state
\sigma^{\theta}
, which is the post-
M
-measurement state. Unlike in nonlocal self-testing, where we only need to characterize one state, here we need to characterize multiple states and observables. Our strategy is to first decompose

\sigma^{\theta}\approx\sum_{v\in\{0,1\}^{2N}}\sigma^{\theta,v},

(4.1)

where
\sigma^{\theta,v}
are (subnormalized) states that correspond to certain
(y,d)
measurement outcomes. We then show that
X_{q,i}
and
Z_{q,i}
act on
\sigma^{\theta,v}
similarly to how
\sigma^{X}_{i}
and
\sigma^{Z}_{i}
act on certain ideal states up to some error that can be bounded by the probability that the device fails our protocol for a given
(\theta,q)
. More specifically, the ideal state corresponding to
\sigma^{\theta,v}
is
\tau^{\theta,v}
as defined in
4.34
. In
Table

1
, we
summarize which
(\theta,q)
pairs are used to characterize which observables. For example,
(\theta,q)=(0,0^{2N})
is used to characterize
Z_{0^{2N},i}
for all
i\in[2N]
while
(\theta,q)=(\diamond,0^{N}1^{N})
is used to characterize
Z_{0^{N}1^{N},i}\cdot X_{0^{N}1^{N},i+N}
for all
i\in[N]
.

(q_{i},q_{i+N})
with
i\leq N

\theta=0

\theta\in[2N]

\theta=\diamond

(0,0)

Z_{q,i}
and
Z_{q,i+N}

-

-

(1,1)

-

X_{q,\theta}
if
\theta\in\{i,i+N\}

-

(0,1)

-

-

Z_{q,i}\cdot X_{q,i+N}

(1,0)

-

-

X_{q,i}\cdot Z_{q,i+N}

Table 1:
Correspondence between the
(\theta,q)
used in our protocol and the observables tested.

Sampling
\theta\leftarrow_{U}[2N]\cup\{0,\diamond\}
and
q
as in our protocol allows us to effectively characterize the actions of
X_{q,i}
and
Z_{q,i}
on
\sigma^{\theta}
for all
(\theta,q,i)\in([2N]\cup\{0,\diamond\})\times\{0^{2N},1^{2N},0^{N}1^{N},1^{N}0^{N}\}\times[2N]
using the overall failure probability of the device. If we naively sampled
\theta\leftarrow_{U}\{0,1\}^{2N}
, we would not be able to effectively bound the failure probability of the device given some
\theta
by the overall failure probability of the device since the former probability can be smaller than the latter by a factor of
2^{2N}
.

Then, in
Proposition

4.14
, we use the computational indistinguishability of elements in
\{\sigma^{\theta}\mid\theta\in[2N]\cup\{0,\diamond\}\}
to argue that, for all
q\in\{0,1\}^{2N}
, the observables
X_{q,i}
and
Z_{q,i}
act on
\sigma^{\theta}
similarly to how
X_{i}\coloneqq X_{1^{2N},i}
and
Z_{i}\coloneqq Z_{0^{2N},i}
act on
\sigma^{\theta}
for all
\theta\in[2N]\cup\{0,\diamond\}
. This allows us to restrict attention for most of the subsequent analysis to
X_{i}
and
Z_{i}
since results that hold for them automatically hold for
X_{q,i}
and
Z_{q,i}
via
Proposition

4.14
.

For self-testing, we not only need to characterize the action of a single observable on
\sigma^{\theta}
as sketched above but we also need to characterize the actions of
products
of observables.
In
Sections

4.3
and
4.4
, we establish the observables’
\sigma^{\theta}
-state-dependent commutation and anti-commutation relations. These relations involve products of two observables. Our proof generalizes and refines techniques used in
[
MV21
,
GV19
]
. In particular, we need to define error parameters associated with each element in
\{\sigma^{\theta,v}\mid v\in\{0,1\}^{2N}\}
. We then use these error parameters collectively to bound the overall approximation error associated with
\sigma^{\theta}
.

In
Section

4.5
, we prove observable-state commutation relations for certain pairs of observables and states. These relations are necessary for showing that products of
more than
two observables, each being either
X_{q,i}
or
Z_{q,i}
, satisfy the same state-dependent (anti-)commutation relations as
\sigma^{Z}_{i}
and
\sigma^{X}_{i}
, respectively. Recall that the previous (anti-)commutation relations only concern products of two observables. However, we also want to show, for example,

Z_{1}X_{3}Z_{2}\sigma^{3}\approx X_{3}Z_{1}Z_{2}\sigma^{3},

(4.2)

for some appropriate approximation error.
Eq.

4.2
does not follow from the
\sigma^{3}
-state-dependent commutation relation between
Z_{1}
and
X_{3}
because
Z_{1}
and
X_{3}
are not directly next to
\sigma^{3}
due to the obstructing
Z_{2}
. However,
Eq.

4.2
does follow once we first use an observable-state commutation relation to commute
Z_{2}
past
\sigma^{3}
. We view our use of observable-state commutation relations as one of the main technical contributions of this work. These techniques should be independently useful in any future work on computational self-testing involving more than two qubits.

In
Section

4.6
, we give the formula of the swap isometry
\mathcal{V}
and then describe an efficient quantum circuit that implements it. Our swap isometry can be viewed as a special case of the swap isometry proposed in
[
YN13
, Figure 2]
in the nonlocal setting. In particular, it is not the obvious generalization of the swap isometry used in
[
MV21
, Proof of Lemma 4.28]
which is more difficult to analyze.

In
Sections

4.7
and
4.8
, we analyze the effect of the swap isometry on the observables and states of the device. More specifically, in
Section

4.7
, we show that
\mathcal{V}
maps the
X_{q,i}
and
Z_{q,i}
observables approximately to
\sigma^{X}_{i}
and
\sigma^{Z}_{i}
.
In
Section

4.8
, we show that
\mathcal{V}
maps the state
\sigma^{\theta,v}
of the device to a state of the form
\tau^{\theta,v}\otimes\alpha^{\theta,v}
, where

\tau^{\theta,v}
is the ideal state and

\alpha^{\theta,v}
is some junk state such that
\alpha^{\theta,v}
is close to being computationally indistinguishable from a fixed state
\alpha
for all
\theta\in[2N]\cup\{0,\diamond\}
and
v\in\{0,1\}^{2N}
.

Lastly, in
Section

4.9
, we put everything together to give our main soundness result,
4.40
. The main task is to characterize the measurement operator
P_{q}=\{P_{q}^{u}\mid u\in\{0,1\}^{2N}\}
, where each
P_{q}^{u}
is approximately a product of
2N
binary projectors of the form
Z_{q,i}^{(u_{i})}
and
X_{q,i}^{(u_{i})}
. Recall that we have
previously characterized
Z_{q,i}
and
X_{q,i}
individually in
Section

4.7
. To characterize the products of their corresponding projectors, we use the operator-state commutation relation to sequentially replace each projector in the product by its ideal counterpart.

The analysis in
Sections

4.7
,
4.8
and
4.9
crucially relies on results in

Sections

4.3
,
4.4
and
4.5
which allow us to bound the soundness error in
4.40
by
O(\mathrm{poly}(N,\epsilon))
.
If we directly generalized the soundness analysis of
[
MV21
]
, we would obtain an extremely loose
O(2^{N}\epsilon^{1/2^{N}})
bound on the soundness error.
4
4

4

See
[
GMP23
, End of Section 1.3 (arXiv version v2)]
for an explanation of why the technique in
[
MV21
]
would lead to such a loose bound.

4.1
Quantum devices

In this subsection, we model a general quantum device
that can be used by a device in our protocol specified in
Section

3
.
Our definition is based on the definition given in

[
MV21
, Section 4.1]
.

Definition 4.1
.

A device
D=(S,M,\Pi,P)
is specified by Hilbert spaces named
\mathcal{H}_{D}
,
\mathcal{H}_{Y}
, and
\mathcal{H}_{R}
, with
\dim(\mathcal{H}_{Y})=\lvert\mathcal{Y}\rvert^{2N}
and
\dim(\mathcal{H}_{R})=2^{2Nw}
, and the following.

1.

A set
S\coloneqq\{\psi^{\theta}\mid\theta\in[2N]\cup\{0,\diamond\}\}\subset\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H}_{D}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{Y})
of states where each state
\psi^{\theta}
is classical on
\mathcal{H}_{Y}
:

\displaystyle\psi^{\theta}\coloneqq\sum_{y\in\mathcal{Y}^{2N}}\psi^{\theta}_{y}\otimes|y\rangle\langle y|.

(4.3)

The state
\psi^{\theta}_{y}
models the device’s state immediately after returning
y\in\mathcal{Y}^{2N}
to the verifier if the verifier initially sampled
\theta\in[2N]\cup\{0,\diamond\}
. More precisely,
\psi^{\theta}_{y}
(and hence
\psi^{\theta}
) is a function of the public keys
k\in(\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{F}}\cup\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{G}})^{2N}
that the verifier sampled according to
\theta
, as described in the protocol. We choose to make the
k
-dependence implicit for notational convenience.

2.

A projective measurement
\Pi

for the preimage test
on
\mathcal{H}_{D}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{Y}
:

\displaystyle\Pi\coloneqq\Biggl\{\Pi^{b,x}\coloneqq\sum_{y\in\mathcal{Y}^{2N}}\Pi_{y}^{b,x}\otimes|y\rangle\langle y|\Biggm|b\in\{0,1\}^{2N},x\in\mathcal{X}^{2N}\Biggr\}.

(4.4)

The measurement outcome
b,x
is the device’s
answer for the preimage test.

3.

A projective measurement
M
on
\mathcal{H}_{D}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{Y}
for the device’s first answer in the Hadamard test:

\displaystyle M\coloneqq\Biggl\{M^{d}\coloneqq\sum_{y\in\mathcal{Y}^{2N}}M_{y}^{d}\otimes|y\rangle\langle y|\Biggm|d\in\{0,1\}^{2Nw}\Biggr\}.

(4.5)

We write
\sigma^{\theta}(D)
for the classical-quantum state that results from measuring
M
on
\psi^{\theta}
followed by writing measurement outcome
d
into another classical register whose Hilbert space is denoted by
\mathcal{H}_{R}
. That is,

\sigma^{\theta}(D)\coloneqq\sum_{y\in\mathcal{Y}^{2N},\,d\in\{0,1\}^{2Nw}}\sigma_{y,d}^{\theta}(D)\otimes|y,d\rangle\langle y,d|\in\mathcal{H}_{D}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{Y}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{R},

(4.6)

where
\sigma_{y,d}^{\theta}(D)\coloneqq M_{y}^{d}\psi^{\theta}_{y}M_{y}^{d}
.

4.

Projective measurements
P_{q}
on
\mathcal{H}_{D}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{Y}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{R}
for the device’s second answer in the Hadamard test when asked question
q\in\{0,1\}^{2N}
:

\displaystyle P_{q}\coloneqq\Biggl\{P_{q}^{u}=\sum_{y\in\mathcal{Y}^{2N},d\in\{0,1\}^{2Nw}}P_{q,y,d}^{u}\otimes|y,d\rangle\langle y,d|\Biggm|u\in\{0,1\}^{2N}\Biggr\}.

(4.7)

The measurement outcome
v
is the device’s answer for the question
q
.

Remark
.

We stress that the states
\psi^{\theta}_{y}
depend on the public keys
k\in(\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{F}}\cup\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{G}})^{2N}
that the verifier sampled according to
\theta
. In particular, all subsequent states of the device also depend on
k
. When we make a statement about such
k
-dependent states labeled by
\theta
, that statement is often understood as holding in expectation over
k
sampled according to
\theta
. The expectation over
k
is necessary when the statement is derived using the adaptive hardcore bit or injective invariance properties of ENTCFs, which only hold in expectation over
k
.

Henceforth, unqualified sums over each of the symbols
b,x,y,d
always refer to sums over
b\in\{0,1\}^{2N}
,
x\in\mathcal{X}^{2N}
,
y\in\mathcal{Y}^{2N}
, and
d\in\{0,1\}^{2Nw}
respectively, unless otherwise stated.

In this work, we focus on efficient quantum devices, which are defined below.

Definition 4.2
.

A device
D=(S,\Pi,M,P)
is efficient
if all the states in
S
can be
efficiently prepared and all the measurements

\Pi,M
, and
P
are efficient.

As in the nonlocal self-testing, we want to show that each projector
P_{q}^{u}
behaves like a tensor product of projectors on
2N
systems. Therefore, we define the marginal observables on each of those systems as done in
[
MV21
, Definition 4.4]
.

Definition 4.3

(Marginal observables)
.

Let
D=(S,\Pi,M,P)
be a device. For
i\in[2N]
and
q\in\{0,1\}^{2N}
, we define the binary observables

\displaystyle Z_{q,i}(D)\coloneqq\sum_{v}(-1)^{v_{i}}P_{q}^{v}\quad\text{if }q_{i}=0\quad\text{and}

(4.8)

\displaystyle X_{q,i}(D)\coloneqq\sum_{v}(-1)^{v_{i}}P_{q}^{v}\quad\text{if }q_{i}=1.

For
i\in[2N]
,
y\in\mathcal{Y}^{2N}
, and
d\in\{0,1\}^{2Nw}
, we also define the binary observables

\displaystyle Z_{q,i,y,d}(D)\coloneqq\sum_{v}(-1)^{v_{i}}P_{q,y,d}^{v}\quad\text{if }q_{i}=0\quad\text{and}

(4.9)

\displaystyle X_{q,i,y,d}(D)\coloneqq\sum_{v}(-1)^{v_{i}}P_{q,y,d}^{v}\quad\text{if }q_{i}=1.

Note that
Z_{q,j}(D)
commutes with
X_{q,k}(D)
for
j\neq k
according to these definitions.

In later parts of this section, we will repeatedly work with
Z_{0^{2N},i}(D)
,
Z_{0^{2N},i,y,d}(D)
,
X_{1^{2N},i}(D)
, and
X_{1^{2N},i,y,d}(D)
, where
i\in[2N]
,
y\in\mathcal{Y}^{2N}
, and
d\in\{0,1\}^{2Nw}
. Therefore, we define the abbreviations

\displaystyle Z_{i}(D)

\displaystyle\coloneqq Z_{0^{2N},i}(D),\quad

\displaystyle Z_{i,y,d}(D)

\displaystyle\coloneqq Z_{0^{2N},i,y,d}(D),

(4.10)

\displaystyle X_{i}(D)

\displaystyle\coloneqq X_{1^{2N},i}(D),\quad

\displaystyle X_{i,y,d}(D)

\displaystyle\coloneqq X_{1^{2N},i,y,d}(D).

We also define the abbreviations

\displaystyle\widetilde{Z}_{i}(D)

\displaystyle\coloneqq Z_{0^{N}1^{N},i}(D)

\displaystyle\text{for all $i\in[N]$},

(4.11)

\displaystyle\widetilde{Z}_{i}(D)

\displaystyle\coloneqq Z_{0^{N}1^{N},i}(D)

\displaystyle\text{for all $i\in\{N+1,\dots,2N\}$},

\displaystyle\widetilde{X}_{i}(D)

\displaystyle\coloneqq X_{1^{N}0^{N},i}(D)

\displaystyle\text{for all $i\in[N]$},

\displaystyle\widetilde{X}_{i}(D)

\displaystyle\coloneqq X_{0^{N}1^{N},i}(D)

\displaystyle\text{for all $i\in\{N+1,\dots,2N\}$}.

Our soundness analysis will characterize the states
\sigma^{\theta,v}
in the following definition as the states that we are self-testing. In the following definition, for
i\in[2N]
, we write

\mathrm{mod}(i+N,2N)\coloneqq\begin{cases}i+N&\text{if $i\leq N$},\\
i-N&\text{if $i>N$}.\end{cases}

(4.12)

Definition 4.4

(Hadamard round post-
d
-measurement states
\sigma^{\theta,v}
)
.

Let
D
be a device. For
\theta\in[2N]\cup\{0,\diamond\}
and
v\in\{0,1\}^{2N}
, we define the state

\sigma^{\theta,v}(D)\coloneqq\sum_{(y,d)\in\mathrm{\Sigma}(\theta,v)}\sigma_{y,d}^{\theta}(D)\otimes\ket{y,d}\bra{y,d}\in\mathcal{H}_{D}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{Y}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{R},

(4.13)

where,

\mathrm{\Sigma}(\theta,v)\coloneqq\begin{cases}\bigl\{(y,d)\bigm|\hat{b}(k_{i},y_{i})=v_{i}\text{ for all }i\neq\theta\text{ and }\hat{h}(k_{\theta},y_{\theta},d_{\theta})=v_{\theta}\oplus v_{\mathrm{mod}(\theta+N,2N)}\bigr\}&\text{if $\theta\in[2N]$},\\
\bigl\{(y,d)\bigm|\hat{b}(k_{i},y_{i})=v_{i}\text{ for all }i\bigr\}&\text{if $\theta=0$},\\
\bigl\{(y,d)\bigm|\hat{h}(k_{i},y_{i},d_{i})=v_{\mathrm{mod}(i+N,2N)}\text{ for all }i\bigr\}&\text{if $\theta=\diamond$.}\end{cases}

(4.14)

In all cases,
(y,d)
ranges over
\mathcal{Y}^{2N}\times\{0,1\}^{2Nw}
,
i
ranges over
[2N]
, and the state
\sigma^{\theta,v}(D)
implicitly depend on keys
k\in(\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{F}}\cup\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{G}})^{2N}
chosen according to
\theta
as described in the protocol.

Note that
\sum_{v}\sigma^{\theta,v}(D)\leq\sigma^{\theta}(D)
by definition because the
\mathrm{\Sigma}(\theta,v)
s partition a subset of
\mathcal{Y}^{2N}\times\{0,1\}^{2Nw}
. In particular, taking traces on both sides gives
\sum_{v}\mathrm{Tr}[\sigma^{\theta,v}(D)]\leq 1
. In the honest case,

\mathrm{Tr}_{Y,R}[\sigma^{\theta,v}(D)]=\begin{cases}2^{-2N}|v_{1}\ldots v_{\theta-1}(-)^{v_{\theta}}v_{\theta+1}\ldots v_{2N}\rangle\langle v_{1}\ldots v_{\theta-1}(-)^{v_{\theta}}v_{\theta+1}\ldots v_{2N}|&\text{for $\theta\in[2N]$,}\\
2^{-2N}|v\rangle\langle v|&\text{for $\theta=0$,}\\
2^{-2N}|\psi^{v}\rangle\langle\psi^{v}|&\text{for $\theta=\diamond$,}\end{cases}

(4.15)

where
\ket{v}\coloneqq\ket{v_{1}v_{2}\ldots v_{2N}}
and we recall the definition of
\ket{\psi^{v}}
from
Eq.

3.5
.

In the soundness proof, we will use quantities called
\gamma_{P}
and
\gamma_{H}
to bound how far away the
Z_{q,i}(D),X_{q,i}(D)
observables and
\sigma^{\theta,v}
states are from the self-tested observables and states. We define them below.

Definition 4.5

(
\gamma_{P}
and
\gamma_{H}
)
.

Let
D
be a device. We define the following quantities that all relate to the failure probabilities of
D
.

1.

Preimage test.
For
\theta\in[2N]\cup\{0,\diamond\}
, we define

\displaystyle t_{\theta}(D)\coloneqq\mathrm{Tr}\Bigl[\sum_{y}\,\sum_{(b,x)\in\mathrm{INV}(k,y)}\Pi^{b,x}_{y}\psi^{\theta}_{y}\Bigr]=\mathrm{Tr}\Bigl[\sum_{y,b,x\mid\mathrm{CHK}(k,y,b,x)=0}\Pi_{y}^{b,x}\psi_{y}^{\theta}\Bigr],

(4.16)

where an implicit expectation is taken over the keys
k\in(\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{F}}\cup\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{G}})^{2N}
that are sampled according to
\theta
as described in the protocol, and

\mathrm{INV}(\theta,y)\coloneqq\{(b,x)\in\{0,1\}^{2N}\times\mathcal{X}^{2N}\,|\,b_{i}=\hat{b}(k_{i},y_{i}),x_{i}=\hat{x}(b_{i},k_{i},y_{i})\text{ for all }i\}.

(4.17)

Note that the set
\mathrm{INV}(\theta,y)
can be empty due to
\hat{b}
or
\hat{x}
returning
\perp
; in that case,
\sum_{(b,x)\in\mathrm{INV}(\theta,y)}\Pi^{b,x}_{y}
is taken to mean
0
. We then define

\gamma_{P}(D)\coloneqq 1-\min\{t_{\theta}(D)\mid\theta\in[2N]\cup\{0,\diamond\}\}.

(4.18)

2.

Hadamard test.
For
\theta\in[2N]\cup\{0\}
,
q\in\{0,1\}^{2N}
and
i\in[2N]
, we define

\displaystyle r_{\theta,q,i}(D)\coloneqq\begin{cases}\mathrm{Tr}\Bigl[\sum_{v}Z_{q,i}^{(v_{i})}\sigma^{\theta,v}\Bigr]&\text{if }q_{i}=0\\
1&\text{otherwise}\end{cases},

(4.19)

\displaystyle s_{\theta,q,i}(D)\coloneqq\begin{cases}\mathrm{Tr}\Bigl[\sum_{v}X_{q,i}^{(v_{i})}\sigma^{\theta,v}\Bigr]&\text{if }q_{i}=1\\
1&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}.

(4.20)

For
\theta=\diamond
and
i\in[N]
, we define

\displaystyle r_{\diamond,q,i}(D)\coloneqq\begin{cases}\sum_{v}\mathrm{Tr}\Bigl[(Z_{q,i}X_{q,N+i})^{(v_{i})}\sigma^{\diamond,v}\Bigr]&\text{if }q_{i}=0\text{ and }q_{N+i}=1\\
1&\text{otherwise}\end{cases},

(4.21)

\displaystyle s_{\diamond,q,i}(D)\coloneqq\begin{cases}\sum_{v}\mathrm{Tr}\Bigl[(X_{q,i}Z_{q,N+i})^{(v_{N+i})}\sigma^{\diamond,v}\Bigr]&\text{if }q_{i}=1\text{ and }q_{N+i}=0\\
1&\text{ otherwise}\end{cases}.

(4.22)

We then define

\gamma_{H,q}(D)\coloneqq 1-\min\{\min\{r_{\theta,q,i},s_{\theta,q,i}\mid\theta\in[2N]\cup\{0\},i\in[2N]\},\min\{r_{\diamond,q,i},s_{\diamond,q,i}\mid i\in[N]\}\}.

(4.23)

Since our soundness proof proceeds by first characterizing the
operators
X_{i}(D),Z_{i}(D),\tilde{X}_{i}(D)
and
\tilde{Z}_{i}(D)
(defined in
Eqs.

4.10
and
4.11
),
we write

\gamma_{H}(D)\coloneqq\max\{\gamma_{H,q}(D)\mid q\in\{0^{2N},1^{2N},0^{N}1^{N},1^{N}0^{N}\}\}.

(4.24)

Henceforth, we assume
5
5

5

This assumption holds when
D
fails our protocol with sufficiently small probability – see
Proposition

4.7
.
that
\gamma_{P}(D),\gamma_{H}(D)<1
only for notational convenience so that we can simplify, for example,
O(\gamma_{H}(D)+\gamma(D)^{r})
to
O(\gamma(D)^{r})
when
0<r<1
. For the same reason, we assume
N\gamma_{H}(D)
is non-negligible in
\lambda
so that we can simplify, for example,
O(N\gamma_{H}(D)+{\mathrm{negl}(\lambda)})
to
O(N\gamma_{H}(D))
.

We define the following failure probabilities of a device, which can be estimated by running the protocol multiple times.

Definition 4.6

(Failure probabilities)
.

Let
D
be a device. For
q\in\{0,1\}^{2N}
, we define

\displaystyle\epsilon_{P}(D)\coloneqq\mathrm{Pr}(D\text{ fails preimage test}\mid\text{ case: preimage}),

(4.25)

\displaystyle\epsilon_{H,q}(D)\coloneqq\mathrm{Pr}(D\text{ fails Hadamard test}\mid\text{ case: Hadamard and question }q).

(4.26)

Then,

\epsilon(D)\coloneqq\epsilon_{P}(D)/2+\Bigl(\sum_{q\in\{0^{2N},1^{2N},0^{N}1^{N},1^{N}0^{N}\}}\frac{1}{4}\epsilon_{H,q}(D)+\sum_{q\in\{0,1\}^{2N}}\mu(q)\epsilon_{H,q}(D)\Bigr)/4.

(4.27)

is the average failure probability.

Henceforth, when
D
is clear from the context,
we mostly drop the
D
-dependence from the quantities

\sigma^{\theta},\sigma^{\theta}_{y,d},\sigma^{\theta,v},Z_{i},X_{i},\widetilde{Z}_{i},\widetilde{X}_{i},Z_{i,y,d},X_{i,y,d},Z_{q,i},X_{q,i},t_{\theta},r_{\theta,q,i},s_{\theta,q,i},\gamma_{H,q},\gamma_{H},\epsilon_{P},\epsilon_{H,q},\epsilon.

(4.28)

While it is easier to bound the soundness error using
\gamma_{P},\gamma_{H,q}
and
\gamma_{H}
, they
are not immediately observable to the verifier. However, we can bound them by the observable failure probabilities,
\epsilon_{P},\epsilon_{H,q},\text{ and }\epsilon
, as follows.

Proposition 4.7

(
\gamma
bounded by failure probability
\epsilon
)
.

Let
D
be a device. Then, for all
q\in\{0,1\}^{2N}
,

\displaystyle\gamma_{P}\leq(2N

\displaystyle+2)\epsilon_{P}\leq 2(2N+2)\epsilon,

(4.29)

\displaystyle\gamma_{H,q}

\displaystyle\leq(2N+2)\epsilon_{H,q}.

(4.30)

Moreover,

\displaystyle\gamma_{H}\coloneqq\max\{\gamma_{H,q}\mid q\in\{0^{2N},1^{2N},0^{N}1^{N},1^{N}0^{N}\}\}\leq 16(2N+2)\epsilon.

(4.31)

Proof.

As
t_{\theta}\leq 1
for all
\theta
,
Eq.

4.29
follows from

\epsilon_{P}=1-\frac{1}{2N+2}\Bigl(t_{\diamond}+\sum_{\theta=0}^{2N}t_{\theta}\Bigr)\geq 1-\frac{1}{2N+2}(2N+1+\min\{t_{\theta}\mid\theta\in[2N]\cup\{0,\diamond\}\})=\frac{\gamma_{P}}{2N+2}.

(4.32)

For
\theta\in[2N]\cup\{0,\diamond\}
and
q\in\{0,1\}^{2N}
, let
p_{\theta,q}
be the success probability of the device when the verifier chooses
\theta
and asks question
q
. Since
\Pr(\mathbin{\scalebox{1.3}{$\cap$}}_{i}A_{i})\leq\min_{i}\{\mathrm{Pr}(A_{i})\}
for any events
A_{i}
, we obtain

\displaystyle p_{0,q}\leq\min\{r_{0,q,i}\mid i\in[2N]\},

(4.33)

\displaystyle p_{\theta,q}\leq\min\{r_{\theta,q,i},s_{\theta,q,\theta}\mid i\in[2N]\}\quad\text{ for }\theta\in[2N],

(4.34)

\displaystyle p_{\diamond,q}\leq\min\set{r_{\diamond,q,i},s_{\diamond,q,i}\mid i\in[N]}.

(4.35)

Recall that

\displaystyle\gamma_{H,q}

\displaystyle=1-\min\set{\min\set{r_{\theta,q,i},s_{\theta,q,i}\mid\theta\in\{0\}\cup[2N],i\in[2N]},\min\set{r_{\diamond,q,i},s_{\diamond,q,i}\mid i\in[N]}}

(4.36)

\displaystyle=1-\min\set{p_{\theta,q}\mid\theta\in[2N]\cup\{0,\diamond\}}.

(4.37)

Then

\displaystyle 1-\epsilon_{H,q}=\frac{1}{2N+2}\sum_{\theta\in[2N]\cup\{0,\diamond\}}p_{\theta,q}\leq\frac{2N+1}{2N+2}+\frac{\min\set{p_{\theta,q}\mid\theta\in[2N]\cup\{0,\diamond\}}}{2N+2},

from which
Eq.

4.30
follows.

Because in the Hadamard test, with probability
1/2
,
q
is chosen from
\set{0^{2N},1^{2N},0^{N}1^{N},1^{N}0^{N}}
uniformly at random, we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}(\epsilon_{H,0^{2N}}+\epsilon_{H,1^{2N}}+\epsilon_{H,0^{N}1^{N}}+\epsilon_{H,1^{N}0^{N}})\leq 4\epsilon.

That is, for
q\in\set{0^{2N},1^{2N},0^{N}1^{N},1^{N}0^{N}}
,
\epsilon_{H,q}\leq 16\epsilon
.
Then
Eq.

4.31
follows from
Eq.

4.30
.
∎

Definition 4.8
.

For
\theta\in[2N]\cup\{0\}
,
q\in\{0,1\}^{2N}
,
i\in[2N]
, and
v\in\{0,1\}^{2N}
, we define

\displaystyle\zeta(\theta,q,i,v)\coloneqq\|Z_{q,i}-(-1)^{v_{i}}I\|_{\sigma^{\theta,v}}^{2}

if
q_{i}=0
,

(4.38)

\displaystyle\chi(\theta,q,i,v)\coloneqq\|X_{q,i}-(-1)^{v_{i}}I\|_{\sigma^{\theta,v}}^{2}

\displaystyle\text{if $q_{i}=1$}.

(4.39)

For
q\in\{0,1\}^{2N}
,
i\in[2N]
, and
v\in\{0,1\}^{2N}
, we define

\displaystyle\zeta_{\diamond}(q,i,v)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\|Z_{q,i}X_{q,N+i}-(-1)^{v_{i}}I\|_{\sigma^{\diamond,v}}^{2}

\displaystyle\text{if $q_{i}=0$ and $q_{N+i}=1$},

(4.40)

\displaystyle\chi_{\diamond}(q,i,v)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\|X_{q,i}Z_{q,N+i}-(-1)^{v_{N+i}}I\|_{\sigma^{\diamond,v}}^{2}

\displaystyle\text{if $q_{i}=1$ and $q_{N+i}=0$}.

(4.41)

The values
\{\zeta,\zeta_{\diamond},\chi,\chi_{\diamond}\}
in the definition above can be viewed as being of order
\gamma_{H,q}/2^{2N}
(see
Lemma

4.10
). We will later bound the errors of various approximate operator-state relations
by sums of
2^{2N}
terms, where each is of the form
\zeta
,
\zeta_{\diamond}
,
\chi
, or
\chi_{\diamond}
.

For convenience, we say an equation involving
\zeta(\theta,q,i,v)
,
\chi(\theta,q,i,v)
,
\zeta_{\diamond}(q,i,v)
, or
\chi_{\diamond}(q,i,v)
holds for all “appropriate”
(\theta,q,i,v)
to mean that the equation holds for all
(\theta,q,i,v)
in the appropriate domain as specified in
4.8
.

For later convenience, for all appropriate
(\theta,i,v)
, we define

\displaystyle\zeta(\theta,i,v)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\zeta(\theta,0^{2N},i,v),\quad

\displaystyle\chi(\theta,v)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\chi(\theta,1^{2N},\theta,v),

(4.42)

\displaystyle\zeta_{\diamond}(i,v)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\zeta_{\diamond}(0^{N}1^{N},i,v),\quad

\displaystyle\chi_{\diamond}(i,v)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\chi_{\diamond}(1^{N}0^{N},i,v).

(4.43)

By the definition of
\simeq
, for all appropriate
(q,i,\theta,v)
, we have

\displaystyle Z_{q,i}

\displaystyle\simeq_{\zeta(\theta,q,i,v),\sigma^{\theta,v}}(-1)^{v_{i}}I

\displaystyle\text{and}\quad X_{q,i}

\displaystyle\simeq_{\chi(\theta,q,i,v),\sigma^{\theta,v}}(-1)^{v_{i}}I,

(4.44)

\displaystyle Z_{q,i}X_{q,N+i}

\displaystyle\simeq_{\zeta_{\diamond}(q,i,v),\sigma^{\diamond,v}}(-1)^{v_{i}}I

\displaystyle\text{and}\quad X_{q,i}Z_{q,N+i}

\displaystyle\simeq_{\chi_{\diamond}(q,i,v),\sigma^{\diamond,v}}(-1)^{v_{N+i}}I.

In addition, by elementary algebra, we can express

\displaystyle\zeta(\theta,q,i,v)/4

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Tr}[\sigma^{\theta,v}]-\mathrm{Tr}[Z_{q,i}^{(v_{i})}\sigma^{\theta,v}]

\displaystyle=\lVert Z_{q,i}^{(v_{i})}-\mathbbm{1}\rVert^{2}_{\sigma^{\theta,v}},

(4.45)

\displaystyle\chi(\theta,q,i,v)/4

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Tr}[\sigma^{\theta,v}]-\mathrm{Tr}[X_{q,i}^{(v_{i})}\sigma^{\theta,v}]

\displaystyle=\lVert X_{q,i}^{(v_{i})}-\mathbbm{1}\rVert^{2}_{\sigma^{\theta,v}},

\displaystyle\zeta_{\diamond}(q,i,v)/4

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Tr}[\sigma^{\diamond,v}]-\mathrm{Tr}[(Z_{q,i}X_{q,N+i})^{(v_{i})}\sigma^{\diamond,v}]

\displaystyle=\lVert(Z_{q,i}X_{q,N+i})^{(v_{i})}-\mathbbm{1}\rVert^{2}_{\sigma^{\diamond,v}},

\displaystyle\chi_{\diamond}(q,i,v)/4

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Tr}[\sigma^{\diamond,v}]-\mathrm{Tr}[(X_{q,i}Z_{q,N+i})^{(v_{N+i})}\sigma^{\diamond,v}]

\displaystyle=\lVert(X_{q,i}Z_{q,N+i})^{(v_{N+i})}-\mathbbm{1}\rVert^{2}_{\sigma^{\diamond,v}}.

The lemma below follows directly from
4.3
and
4.5
.

Lemma 4.9
.

For
\theta\in[2N]\cup\{0\}
,
q\in\{0,1\}^{2N}
, and
i\in[2N]
such that
q_{i}=0
and
i\neq\theta
, we have

1-\gamma_{H,q}\leq r_{\theta,q,i}=\sum_{v}\mathrm{Tr}[Z_{q,i}^{(v_{i})}\sigma^{\theta,v}]\leq\sum_{v}\mathrm{Tr}[\sigma^{\theta,v}]\leq\mathrm{Tr}[\sigma^{\theta}]=1.

(4.46)

For
\theta\in[2N]
and
q\in\{0,1\}^{2N}
such that
q_{\theta}=1
,
we have

1-\gamma_{H,q}\leq s_{\theta,q,\theta}=\sum_{v}\mathrm{Tr}[X_{q,\theta}^{(v_{\theta})}\sigma^{\theta,v}]\leq\sum_{v}\mathrm{Tr}[\sigma^{\theta,v}]\leq\mathrm{Tr}[\sigma^{\theta}]=1.

(4.47)

For
q\in\{0,1\}^{2N}
and
i\in[N]
such that
q_{i}=0
and
q_{N+i}=1
, we have

\displaystyle 1-\gamma_{H,q}

\displaystyle\leq r_{\diamond,q,i}

\displaystyle=\sum_{v}\mathrm{Tr}[(Z_{q,i}X_{q,N+i})^{(v_{i})}\sigma^{\diamond,v}]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{v}\mathrm{Tr}[\sigma^{\theta,v}]

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{Tr}[\sigma^{\theta}]

\displaystyle=1.

(4.48)

For
q\in\{0,1\}^{2N}
and
i\in[N]
such that
q_{i}=1
and
q_{N+i}=0
, we have

\displaystyle 1-\gamma_{H,q}

\displaystyle\leq s_{\diamond,q,i}

\displaystyle=\sum_{v}\mathrm{Tr}[(X_{q,i}Z_{q,N+i})^{(v_{N+i})}\sigma^{\diamond,v}]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{v}\mathrm{Tr}[\sigma^{\theta,v}]

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{Tr}[\sigma^{\theta}]

\displaystyle=1.

(4.49)

The following lemma bounds exponential sums of
\zeta(\theta,q,i,v)
,
\chi(\theta,q,\theta,v)
,
\chi_{\diamond}(q,i,v)
, and
\zeta_{\diamond}(q,i,v)
over
v\in\{0,1\}^{2N}
using
\gamma_{H,q}
.

Lemma 4.10
.

For all appropriate
(\theta,q,i,v)
with
i\neq\theta
, we have

\sum_{v}\zeta(\theta,q,i,v)\leq 4\gamma_{H,q}.

(4.50)

For all appropriate
(\theta,q,i,v)
with
i=\theta
, we have

\sum_{v}\chi(\theta,q,i,v)\leq 4\gamma_{H,q}.

(4.51)

For all appropriate
(q,i,v)
, we have

\sum_{v}\zeta_{\diamond}(q,i,v)\leq 4\gamma_{H,q}\quad\text{and}\quad\sum_{v}\chi_{\diamond}(q,i,v)\leq 4\gamma_{H,q}.

(4.52)

In particular, we have

\sum_{v}\zeta(\theta,i,v)\leq 4\gamma_{H},\quad\sum_{v}\chi(\theta,v)\leq 4\gamma_{H},\quad\sum_{v}\zeta_{\diamond}(i,v)\leq 4\gamma_{H},\quad\text{and}\quad\sum_{v}\chi_{\diamond}(i,v)\leq 4\gamma_{H}.

(4.53)

Proof.

Consider the first inequality. Fix
\theta\in[2N]\cup\{0\}
,
q\in\{0,1\}^{2N}
, and
i\in[2N]
with
q_{i}=0
and
i\neq\theta
. Summing the expression for
\zeta(\theta,q,i,v)
in
Eq.

4.45
over
v\in\{0,1\}^{2N}
and using
Lemma

4.9
, we obtain

\sum_{v}\zeta(\theta,q,i,v)\leq 4-4\sum_{v}\mathrm{Tr}[Z_{q,i}^{(v_{i})}\sigma^{\theta,v}]\leq 4\gamma_{H,q}.

(4.54)

The argument is analogous for the remaining three inequalities before the “in particular” part.

The “in particular” part follows from the definitions of
\zeta(\theta,i,v)
,
\chi(\theta,v)
,
\zeta_{\diamond}(i,v)
, and
\chi_{\diamond}(i,v)
in
Eq.

4.42
together with the definition of
\gamma_{H}
in
Eq.

4.24
.
∎

Note that the above lemmas control certain parameters of a device using its failure probability. But we have yet to leverage our computational assumptions. The following lemma, which we will frequently use later together with the lifting lemmas (
Lemmas

2.16
and
2.17
), allows us to use the injective invariance property of ENTCFs (
(e)
) to further control the device.

Lemma 4.11

(Indistinguishability of
\{\psi^{\theta}\}_{\theta}

(\mathrm{resp.}~\{\sigma^{\theta}\}_{\theta})
)
.

Any pair of states in
\{\psi^{\theta}\}_{\theta\in[2N]\cup\{0,\diamond\}}

(\mathrm{resp.}~\{\sigma^{\theta}\}_{\theta\in[2N]\cup\{0,\diamond\}})
of an efficient device
D
are computationally indistinguishable.

Proof.

For
v\in\{0,1\}^{2N}
, let
\mathcal{D}(v)\coloneqq(k_{1},k_{2},\ldots,k_{2N})
be the distribution on
2N
-tuples of public keys such that each
k_{i}

is independently distributed according to
\mathrm{Gen}_{\mathcal{G}}(1^{\lambda})_{\mathrm{key}}
if
v_{i}=0
or
\mathrm{Gen}_{\mathcal{F}}(1^{\lambda})_{\mathrm{key}}
if
v_{i}=1
.

For
\theta\in[2N]\cup\{0,\diamond\}
, let

\mathcal{D}^{\theta}\coloneqq\begin{cases}\mathcal{D}(\mathrm{str}(\theta))&\text{if $\theta\in[2N]$,}\\
\mathcal{D}(0^{2N})&\text{if $\theta=0$,}\\
\mathcal{D}(1^{2N})&\text{if $\theta=\diamond$,}\end{cases}

(4.55)

where
\mathrm{str}(\theta)\in\{0,1\}^{2N}
is the
2N
-bit string with a
1
at position
\theta
and
0
s elsewhere.

The injective invariance property of ENTCFs (
(e)
) states that the distributions
\mathrm{Gen}_{\mathcal{G}}(1^{\lambda})_{\mathrm{key}}
and
\mathrm{Gen}_{\mathcal{F}}(1^{\lambda})_{\mathrm{key}}
are computationally indistinguishable. Therefore, it is clear that for any
u,v\in\{0,1\}^{2N}
, with
u,v
differing by exactly one bit,
\mathcal{D}(u)
and
\mathcal{D}(v)
are computationally indistinguishable. Therefore, any pair of distributions in
\{\mathcal{D}^{\theta}\}_{\theta\in[2N]\cup\{0,\diamond\}}
are computationally indistinguishable up to
2N\,{\mathrm{negl}(\lambda)}={\mathrm{negl}(\lambda)}
by
Lemma

2.13
and our setting
N=\lambda
. This is because, for any
\theta,\theta^{\prime}\in[2N]\cup\{0\}
,
\mathrm{str}(\theta)
and
\mathrm{str}(\theta^{\prime})
differ by at most
2
bits while
\mathrm{str}(\theta)
and
1^{2N}
differ by at most
2N
bits. As
D
can prepare the state
\psi^{\theta}
(resp.
\sigma^{\theta}
) efficiently given keys drawn from
\mathcal{D}^{\theta}
, all pairs of states in
\{\psi^{\theta}\}_{\theta\in[2N]\cup\{0,\diamond\}}
(resp.
\{\sigma^{\theta}\}_{\theta\in[2N]\cup\{0,\diamond\}}
) must be computationally indistinguishable.
∎

In later parts of the soundness proof, we use
\gamma_{P}
,
\gamma_{H,q}
and
\gamma_{H}
to characterize
\sigma^{\theta}
-dependent operator relations.
First, we show
\sum_{v}\sigma^{\theta,v}
is
\gamma_{P}
-close to
\sigma^{\theta}
so that in later proofs we can replace

\sigma^{\theta}
by
\sum_{v}\sigma^{\theta,v}
, which is easier to analyze—see
4.8
and the lemmas following it.

Definition 4.12

(Valid
y
)
.

Let
y\in\mathcal{Y}^{2N}
and
k\in(\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{F}}\cup\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{G}})^{2N}
. We say
y
is valid (with respect to
k
) if
\hat{b}(k_{i},y_{i})\neq\perp
for all
i\in[2N]
; equivalently,

y_{i}\in\bigcup_{x}\bigl(\Supp(f_{k_{i},0}(x))\cup\Supp(f_{k_{i},1}(x))\bigr),\quad\text{for all $i\in[2N]$.}

(4.56)

Otherwise, we say
y
is invalid.

Lemma 4.13
.

Let
D
be a device. For all
\theta\in[2N]\cup\{0,\diamond\}
,
\lVert\sigma^{\theta}-\sum_{v}\sigma^{\theta,v}\rVert_{1}\leq\gamma_{P}
.

Proof.

We have

\displaystyle\Bigl\lVert\sigma^{\theta}-\sum_{v}\sigma^{\theta,v}\Bigr\rVert_{1}=

\displaystyle\Bigl\lVert\sum_{\text{invalid }y}\;\sum_{d}M_{y}^{d}\psi_{y}^{\theta}M_{y}^{d}\otimes|y,d\rangle\langle y,d|\Bigr\rVert_{1}

(4.57)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Bigl|\mathrm{Tr}\Bigl[\sum_{\text{invalid }y}\;\sum_{d}M_{y}^{d}\psi_{y}^{\theta}M_{y}^{d}\otimes|y,d\rangle\langle y,d|\Bigr]\Bigr|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\text{invalid }y}\mathrm{Tr}[\psi_{y}^{\theta}]\leq 1-t_{\theta}\leq\gamma_{P},

where the last two inequalities follow from the definitions of
t_{\theta}
and
\gamma_{P}
respectively.
∎

Lemmas

4.10
,
4.11
and
4.13
imply that, on the state
\sigma^{\theta}
,
Z_{q,i}
is close to
Z_{i}\coloneqq Z_{0^{2N},i}
and
X_{q,i}
is close to
X_{i}\coloneqq X_{1^{2N},i}
. More precisely:

Proposition 4.14
.

For all
q\in\{0,1\}^{2N}
,
\theta\in[2N]\cup\{0,\diamond\}
, and
i\in[2N]
, we have

\displaystyle Z_{q,i}\simeq_{8\gamma_{H,q}+8\gamma_{H}+4\gamma_{P},\sigma^{\theta}}Z_{i}

\displaystyle\text{if $q_{i}=0$,}

(4.58)

\displaystyle X_{q,i}\simeq_{8\gamma_{H,q}+8\gamma_{H}+4\gamma_{P},\sigma^{\theta}}X_{i}

\displaystyle\text{if $q_{i}=1$.}

Proof.

We first prove the equation involving
Z
. It suffices to prove this equation for
\theta=0
because of the computational indistinguishability of the
\sigma^{\theta}
s (
Lemma

4.11
) and part 2 of the lifting lemma (
Lemma

2.16
). Let
q\in\{0,1\}^{2N}
and
i\in[2N]
be such that
q_{i}=0
. Then, we have

\displaystyle\lVert Z_{q,i}-Z_{i}\rVert^{2}_{\sigma^{0,v}}

\displaystyle\leq(\lVert Z_{q,i}-(-1)^{v_{i}}I\rVert_{\sigma^{0,v}}+\lVert Z_{i}-(-1)^{v_{i}}I\rVert_{\sigma^{0,v}})^{2}

(4.59)

\displaystyle\leq 2\zeta(0,q,i,v)+2\zeta(0,0^{2N},i,v).

Therefore, by
Lemma

2.5
and the bounds on
\zeta
in
Lemma

4.10
, which is applicable because
i\neq 0
, we obtain

Z_{q,i}\simeq_{8\gamma_{H,q}+8\gamma_{H,0^{2N}},\sum_{v}\sigma^{0,v}}Z_{i}.

(4.60)

Now, using
Lemma

4.13
, we obtain

\Bigl|\mathrm{Tr}[(Z_{q,i}-Z_{i})^{2}\sigma^{0}]-\mathrm{Tr}[(Z_{q,i}-Z_{i})^{2}\sum_{v}\sigma^{0,v}]\Bigr|\leq\|Z_{q,i}-Z_{i}\|_{\infty}^{2}\cdot\gamma_{P}\leq 4\gamma_{P}.

(4.61)

Therefore, by
Eq.

4.60
, we have

\mathrm{Tr}[(Z_{q,i}-Z_{i})^{2}\sigma^{0}]\leq\mathrm{Tr}[(Z_{q,i}-Z_{i})^{2}\sum_{v}\sigma^{0,v}]+4\gamma_{P}\leq 8\gamma_{H,q}+8\gamma_{H,0^{2N}}+4\gamma_{P},

(4.62)

which, noting
\gamma_{H,0^{2N}}\leq\gamma_{H}
by definition, completes the proof of the first equation of the lemma.

Now consider the second equation of the lemma involving
X
. It again suffices to only consider
\theta=i
by the reasoning at the start. The proof is then analogous to the proof of the first equation, except we use the bounds on
\chi
in
Lemma

4.10
, which is applicable because
\theta=i
. We omit the details.
∎

4.2
Reduction to perfect device

In this subsection, we follow the strategy of
[
MV21
, Lemma 4.13]
by first showing
that any efficient device with
\gamma_{P}(D)<1
is close to a “perfect” efficient device. A perfect device is one that, for any
\theta\in[2N]\cup\{0,\diamond\}
chosen by the verifier, can always pass the preimage test except with
{\mathrm{negl}(\lambda)}
small probability.
Then, we restrict attention to efficient perfect devices for the rest of the soundness proof before
Section

4.9
.

Definition 4.15

(Perfect device)
.

Let
D=(S,\Pi,M,P)
be an device. We say
D
is perfect if
\gamma_{P}(D)={\mathrm{negl}(\lambda)}
.

Proposition 4.16
.

Let
D=(S,\Pi,M,P)
be an efficient device with
\gamma_{P}(D)<1
and
S=\{\psi^{\theta}\mid\theta\in[2N]\cup\{0,\diamond\}\}
.
Then, there exists an efficient perfect device
\widetilde{D}=(\widetilde{S},\Pi,M,P)
which uses the same measurements
\Pi,M,P
as
D
and has states
\widetilde{S}=\{\widetilde{\psi}^{\theta}\mid\theta\in[2N]\cup\{0,\diamond\}\}
that satisfy: for all
\theta\in[2N]\cup\{0,\diamond\}
,

\lVert\psi^{\theta}-\widetilde{\psi}^{\theta}\rVert_{1}\leq\sqrt{\gamma_{P}(D)}.

(4.63)

Proof.

\widetilde{D}
can efficiently prepare each state
\widetilde{\psi}^{\theta}
in
\widetilde{S}
as follows.
\widetilde{D}
first follows
D
to prepare
\psi^{\theta}
using a given set of public keys
k\in(\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{F}}\cup\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{G}})^{2N}
sampled according to
\theta
as described in the protocol.
\widetilde{D}
then applies the efficient unitary
U_{\Pi}
associated with the efficient measurement
\Pi
, as per
2.1
, to create the state

\phi^{\theta}\coloneqq U_{\Pi}(|0_{2N+2Nw}\rangle\langle 0_{2N+2Nw}|_{\mathrm{anc}}\otimes\psi^{\theta})U_{\Pi}^{\dagger}=\sum_{y,b,x,b^{\prime},x^{\prime}}\ket{y,b,x}\bra{y,b^{\prime},x^{\prime}}\otimes\Pi^{b,x}_{y}\psi_{y}^{\theta}\Pi^{b^{\prime},x^{\prime}}_{y}.

(4.64)

On this state,
\widetilde{D}
evaluates the

\mathrm{CHK}
function—which is efficient given only
k
and not the trapdoor
t_{k}
, see the efficient decoding property of ENTCFs (
(f)
)—on the classical register holding
(y,b,x)
to create the state

\sum_{y,b,x,b^{\prime},x^{\prime}}\ket{\mathrm{CHK}(k,y,b,x)}\bra{\mathrm{CHK}(k,y,b^{\prime},x^{\prime})}\otimes\ket{y,b,x}\bra{y,b^{\prime},x^{\prime}}\otimes\Pi^{b,x}_{y}\psi_{y}^{\theta}\Pi^{b^{\prime},x^{\prime}}_{y}.

(4.65)

\widetilde{D}
next measures the first (single-bit) register of the state in
Eq.

4.65
.
The probability that the measurement outcome is
0
is equal to
\mathrm{Tr}[\Lambda\phi^{\theta}]
, where we write
\Lambda
for the projector

\Lambda\coloneqq\sum_{y}\sum_{b,x\mid\mathrm{CHK}(k,b,x,y)=0}|y,b,x\rangle\langle y,b,x|.

(4.66)

Note that
\mathrm{Tr}[\Lambda\phi^{\theta}]=t_{\theta}
, where we recall
t_{\theta}
from
4.5
.

If the measurement outcome is
0
,
\widetilde{D}
continues by applying
U_{\Pi}^{\dagger}
to the post-measurement states, and then traces out the
\mathrm{anc}
register to output the state

\widetilde{\psi}^{\theta}\coloneqq\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathrm{anc}}\Bigg[U_{\Pi}^{\dagger}\,\frac{\Lambda\phi^{\theta}\Lambda}{\mathrm{Tr}[\Lambda\phi^{\theta}]}\,U_{\Pi}\Bigg].

(4.67)

But, using
Eq.

4.64
, we can write

\psi^{\theta}=\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathrm{anc}}\Big[U_{\Pi}^{\dagger}\phi^{\theta}U_{\Pi}\Big].

(4.68)

Therefore, since the trace norm cannot increase under partial trace and is unitarily invariant, we obtain

\|\widetilde{\psi}^{\theta}-\psi^{\theta}\|_{1}\leq\Bigl\lVert\frac{\Lambda\phi^{\theta}\Lambda}{\mathrm{Tr}[\Lambda\phi^{\theta}]}-\phi^{\theta}\Bigr\rVert_{1}\leq 2\sqrt{1-\mathrm{Tr}[\Lambda\phi^{\theta}]}=\sqrt{1-t_{\theta}}\leq\sqrt{\gamma_{P}},

(4.69)

where we used the gentle measurement lemma in the second inequality, see, e.g.,
[
Wil17
, Lemma 9.4.1 in arXiv v8]
.

Now,
\widetilde{D}
might not obtain
0
when it measures the first register of the state in
Eq.

4.65
. In this case, it repeats the above procedure up to (a fixed)
\mathrm{poly}(\lambda)
times, stopping and outputting
\widetilde{\psi}^{\theta}
the first time
0
is measured and aborting if
0
is never measured within those
\mathrm{poly}(\lambda)
repeats. But, the probability of aborting is
(1-t_{\theta})^{\mathrm{poly}(\lambda)}\leq\gamma_{P}^{\mathrm{poly}(\lambda)}={\mathrm{negl}(\lambda)}
as required.
∎

We finish this subsection by proving several lemmas that hold for efficient perfect devices.
Lemmas

4.17
and
4.18
are used implicitly in
[
MV21
]
. The other
Lemmas

4.19
and
4.20
are generalizations of explicit lemmas in
[
MV21
]
and provide a precise reference in their proofs.

Lemma 4.17
.

Let
D
be a perfect device. For all
\theta\in[2N]\cup\{0,\diamond\}
, we have

\sigma^{\theta}\simeq_{{\mathrm{negl}(\lambda)}}\sum_{v}\sigma^{\theta,v}.

(4.70)

Proof.

The lemma follows from
Lemma

4.13
and the definition of a perfect device (
4.15
).
∎

Lemma 4.18

(Collapsing property)
.

Let
D
be an efficient perfect device. For all
i\in[2N]
and
\theta\in[2N]\cup\{0,\diamond\}
, we have

\displaystyle\psi^{\theta}\overset{c}{\simeq}_{{\mathrm{negl}(\lambda)}}\sum_{b,x}\Pi^{b,x}\psi^{\theta}\Pi^{b,x}.

Proof.

For
c\in\{0,1\}
, let

\mathcal{Y}_{i,c}\coloneqq\{y_{i}\in\mathcal{Y}\mid\hat{b}(k_{i},y_{i})=c\}
. Note that
y\in\mathcal{Y}^{2N}
is valid (see
4.12
) if and only if
y_{i}\in\mathcal{Y}_{i,0}\cup\mathcal{Y}_{i,1}
for all
i\in[2N]
.

We first prove the lemma in the case
\theta=0
, so
k\in\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{G}}^{2N}
. As the device is perfect and
\theta=0
, with probability
\geq 1-{\mathrm{negl}(\lambda)}
, we have

\psi_{y}^{0}=\begin{cases}0&\text{for all invalid $y\in\mathcal{Y}^{2N}$,}\\
\Pi_{y}^{\hat{b}(k,y),\hat{x}(k,y)}\psi^{0}_{y}\Pi_{y}^{\hat{b}(k,y),\hat{x}(k,y)}&\text{for all valid $y\in\mathcal{Y}^{2N}$.}\end{cases}

(4.71)

Therefore, with probability
\geq 1-{\mathrm{negl}(\lambda)}
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{b,x}\Pi^{b,x}\psi^{0}\Pi^{b,x}

\displaystyle=\sum_{b,x,y}\Pi^{b,x}_{y}\psi^{0}_{y}\Pi^{b,x}_{y}\otimes|y\rangle\langle y|

\displaystyle=\sum_{\text{valid }y}\Pi_{y}^{\hat{b}(k,y),\hat{x}(k,y)}\psi^{0}_{y}\Pi_{y}^{\hat{b}(k,y),\hat{x}(k,y)}\otimes|y\rangle\langle y|

\displaystyle=\sum_{\text{valid }y}\psi_{y}^{0}\otimes|y\rangle\langle y|=\sum_{y}\psi_{y}^{0}\otimes|y\rangle\langle y|=\psi^{0}.

When
\theta\neq 0
, the computational indistinguishability of the
\psi^{\theta}
s (
Lemma

4.11
) implies

\displaystyle\sum_{b,x}\Pi^{b,x}\psi^{\theta}\Pi^{b,x}\overset{c}{\simeq}_{{\mathrm{negl}(\lambda)}}\sum_{b,x}\Pi^{b,x}\psi^{0}\Pi^{b,x}\simeq_{{\mathrm{negl}(\lambda)}}\psi^{0}\overset{c}{\simeq}_{{\mathrm{negl}(\lambda)}}\psi^{\theta}.

(4.72)

The conclusion follows by the triangle inequality for computational indistinguishability (
Lemma

2.13
).
∎

Lemma 4.19
.

Let
D
be an efficient perfect device. For all
\theta\in[2N]
, we have

\sum_{v}(-1)^{v_{\theta}}\mathrm{Tr}[\sigma^{\theta,v}]\simeq_{{\mathrm{negl}(\lambda)}}0.

(4.73)

Proof.

The proof is similar to
[
MV21
, Lemma 4.15]
. It suffices to prove the lemma for
\theta=1
as the proof for other
\theta
is analogous. Assume for contradiction that
Eq.

4.73
is not true. Then, there exists a non-negligible function
{\mu(\lambda)}
such that, for infinitely many
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
, we have

\Bigl|\sum_{v|v_{1}=0}\mathrm{Tr}[\sigma^{1,v}]-\sum_{v|v_{1}=1}\mathrm{Tr}[\sigma^{1,v}]\Bigr|>{\mu(\lambda)}.

(4.74)

We also assume that, for a fixed
\lambda
, the term inside the absolute value sign in
Eq.

4.74
is
\geq 0
. The proof is analogous if otherwise.

Consider the following efficient algorithm
\mathcal{A}
which breaks the adaptive hardcore bit property of ENTCFs (
(d)
):

1.

Input: a key
k_{1}
sampled from
\mathrm{Gen}_{\mathcal{F}}(1^{\lambda})_{\mathrm{key}}
.

2.

Independently sample
2N-1
keys
k_{2},\ldots,k_{2N}
, each from
\mathrm{Gen}_{\mathcal{G}}(1^{\lambda})_{\mathrm{key}}
. (Let
\mathcal{D}^{1}
denote the distribution on the keys sampled in the first two steps of
\mathcal{A}
.)

3.

Prepare the state
\psi^{1}
, then make measurement obtaining
y
, then make preimage measurement obtaining
(b,x)
, and then make Hadamard measurement obtaining
d
.

4.

Output:
(y_{1},b_{1},x_{1},d_{1})
and print “
f_{k_{1},b_{1}}(x_{1})=y_{1}\text{ and }\hat{h}(k_{1},y_{1},d_{1})=0
”.

Because
D
is perfect, we have, with probability
\geq 1-{\mathrm{negl}(\lambda)}
,

f_{k_{1},b_{1}}(x_{1})=y_{1}.

(4.75)

Let
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
be the same as
\mathcal{A}
except that it does not make a preimage measurement and produces no output. By the collapsing property (
Lemma

4.18
), the state of
\mathcal{A}
immediately after the preimage measurement is computationally indistinguishable from its state just before. Therefore, by definition, the state of
\mathcal{A}
after the Hadamard measurement is computationally indistinguishable from the state of
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
after the Hadamard measurement. But note that
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
behaves exactly like
D
up to when the Hadamard measurement is made in the Hadamard case. Therefore, the state of
\mathcal{A}
after the Hadamard measurement is computationally indistinguishable from the state of
D
after the Hadamard measurement in the Hadamard case.

Now, using the definitions of
\sigma^{1,v}
and
\mathrm{\Sigma}(1,v)
from
4.4
, we see that
Eq.

4.74
means

\mathrm{Pr}_{k\sim\mathcal{D}^{1},D}\bigl((y,d)\in\mathbin{\scalebox{1.3}{$\cup$}}_{v|v_{1}=0}\mathrm{\Sigma}(1,v)\bigr)>\mathrm{Pr}_{k\sim\mathcal{D}^{1},D}\bigl((y,d)\in\mathbin{\scalebox{1.3}{$\cup$}}_{v|v_{1}=1}\mathrm{\Sigma}(1,v)\bigr)+{\mu(\lambda)}.

(4.76)

Then, as argued above, we can replace
D
by
\mathcal{A}
by introducing a negligible term as follows

\mathrm{Pr}_{k\sim\mathcal{D}^{1},\mathcal{A}}\bigl((y,d)\in\mathbin{\scalebox{1.3}{$\cup$}}_{v|v_{1}=0}\mathrm{\Sigma}(1,v)\bigr)>\mathrm{Pr}_{k\sim\mathcal{D}^{1},\mathcal{A}}\bigl((y,d)\in\mathbin{\scalebox{1.3}{$\cup$}}_{v|v_{1}=1}\mathrm{\Sigma}(1,v)\bigr)+{\mu(\lambda)}+{\mathrm{negl}(\lambda)}.

(4.77)

Now, we can rewrite the probabilities appearing on the left- and right-hand sides to see that

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pr}\bigl(\hat{h}(k_{1},y_{1},d_{1})=0\text{ and }\mathbin{\scalebox{1.3}{$\cap$}}_{i=2}^{2N}(\hat{b}(k_{i},y_{i})\in\{0,1\})\bigr)

(4.78)

\displaystyle>\mathrm{Pr}\bigl(\hat{h}(k_{1},y_{1},d_{1})=1\text{ and }\mathbin{\scalebox{1.3}{$\cap$}}_{i=2}^{2N}(\hat{b}(k_{i},y_{i})\in\{0,1\})\bigr)+{\mu(\lambda)}+{\mathrm{negl}(\lambda)}.

But

\mathrm{Pr}\bigl(\mathbin{\scalebox{1.3}{$\cap$}}_{i=2}^{2N}\,(\hat{b}(k_{i},y_{i})\in\{0,1\})\bigr)\geq 1-{\mathrm{negl}(\lambda)},

(4.79)

by the definition of
\hat{b}
and the fact
D
is perfect. Therefore, as
\mathrm{Pr}(A\cap B)\geq\mathrm{Pr}(A)+\mathrm{Pr}(B)-1
for any events
A
and
B
, we have

~\mathrm{Pr}(\hat{h}(k_{1},y_{1},d_{1})=0)>\mathrm{Pr}(\hat{h}(k_{1},y_{1},d_{1})=1)+{\mu(\lambda)}+{\mathrm{negl}(\lambda)}.

(4.80)

Eqs.

4.75
and
4.80
together contradict the adaptive hardcore bit property of ENTCFs (
(d)
).
∎

Lemma 4.20
.

Let
D
be an efficient perfect device. For all
i,\theta\in[2N]
, we have

\mathrm{Tr}[X_{i}\sigma^{\theta}]\simeq_{2\gamma_{H}+{\mathrm{negl}(\lambda)}}0.

(4.81)

Proof.

The proof is similar to
[
MV21
, Corollary 4.16]
. Because
D
is efficient,
X_{i}
is an efficient binary observable. Therefore, it suffices to consider
\theta=i
by using the computational indistinguishability of the
\sigma^{\theta}
s (
Lemma

4.11
) and part
1
of the lifting lemma (
Lemma

2.16
). Moreover, we only consider
\theta=i=1
as the argument is analogous otherwise. Then, the lemma follows from

\mathrm{Tr}[X_{1}\sigma^{1}]=\sum_{v}\mathrm{Tr}[(-1)^{v_{1}}(2X_{1}^{(v_{1})}-\mathbbm{1})\sigma^{1,v}]=\sum_{v}(-1)^{v_{1}}\mathrm{Tr}[\sigma^{1,v}]-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{v}(-1)^{v_{1}}\chi(1,v)\simeq_{2\gamma_{H}}{\mathrm{negl}(\lambda)},

(4.82)

where the middle equality uses
Eq.

4.45
and the last approximation uses
Lemmas

4.10
and
4.19
.
∎

4.3
Commutation relations

Proposition 4.21
.

Let
D
be an efficient perfect device. For all
i,j,\theta\in[2N]
with
i\neq j
, we have

[Z_{i},Z_{j}]=0,\quad[X_{i},X_{j}]=0,\quad\mathrm{and}\quad[Z_{i},X_{j}]\approx_{\gamma_{H}+{\mathrm{negl}(\lambda)},\sigma^{\theta}}0.

(4.83)

Our proof is similar to that of
[
MV21
, Proposition 4.24]
except it is more refined in that we use
\zeta_{v}
and
\chi_{v}
to bound various
\sigma^{\theta,v}
-dependent approximation errors instead of
\gamma_{H}
. For example, in
Eq.

4.84
, We cannot use
\gamma_{H}
to bound the errors as it would lead to a
O(2^{2N}\gamma_{H})
approximation error in
Eq.

4.83
due to
Eq.

4.85
where a sum over
v\in\{0,1\}^{2N}
is taken.

Proof.

The first two equations follow directly from
4.3
. Consider the last equation. Because
D
is efficient,
Z_{i}
and
X_{j}
are efficient binary observables. Therefore, by the computational indistinguishability of the
\sigma^{\theta}
s (
Lemma

4.11
) and part
3
of the lifting lemma (
Lemma

2.16
), it suffices to prove the last equation for
\theta=j
. It also suffices to consider
i=1
and
j=\theta=2
as the proof is analogous for other
i,j,\theta
with
i\neq j
.

For the purposes of this proof, we write
\zeta_{v}
for
\zeta(2,1,v)
and
\chi_{v}
for
\chi(2,v)
for convenience. By definitions, we have

Z_{1}\approx_{\zeta_{v},\sigma^{2,v}}(-1)^{v_{1}}I
and
X_{2}\approx_{\chi_{v},\sigma^{2,v}}(-1)^{v_{2}}I
. Therefore,

Z_{1}X_{2}\approx_{\chi_{v},\sigma^{2,v}}(-1)^{v_{2}}Z_{1}\approx_{\zeta_{v},\sigma^{2,v}}(-1)^{v_{2}+v_{1}}I\approx_{\chi_{v},\sigma^{2,v}}X_{2}\,(-1)^{v_{1}}\approx_{\zeta_{v},\sigma^{2,v}}X_{2}Z_{1}.

(4.84)

Therefore, by the triangle inequality, we have

Z_{1}X_{2}\approx_{\chi_{v}+\zeta_{v},\sigma^{2,v}}X_{2}Z_{1}.

By linearity of the trace we can sum the above equation up over
v\in\{0,1\}^{2N}
to deduce

Z_{1}X_{2}\approx_{\sum_{v}{\chi_{v}+\zeta_{v}},\sum_{v}\sigma^{2,v}}X_{2}Z_{1}.

(4.85)

Using
\sum_{v}\chi_{v}\leq 4\gamma_{H}
and
\sum_{v}\zeta_{v}\leq 4\gamma_{H}
from
Lemma

4.10
, and using
\sigma^{2}\simeq_{{\mathrm{negl}(\lambda)}}\sum_{v}\sigma^{2,v}
from
Lemma

4.17
, which uses the perfectness of
D
, we obtain

Z_{1}X_{2}\approx_{\gamma_{H}+{\mathrm{negl}(\lambda)},\sigma^{2}}X_{2}Z_{1},

(4.86)

which completes the proof.
∎

4.4
Anti-commutation relations

We prove
Proposition

4.22
in this subsection. The results and their proofs in this subsection are generalizations of those in
[
MV21
, Section 4.6]
.
More specifically,
[
MV21
]
deals with the two-qubit case and proves results for
\sigma^{(\theta_{1},\theta_{2})}
with
(\theta_{1},\theta_{2})\in\{0,1\}^{2}
.
The naive generalization to the
2N
-qubit case would consider
(\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{2N})\in\{0,1\}^{2N}
. However, such generalization would not allow us to effectively control
\gamma_{H}
using the failure probability
\epsilon_{H,q}
, since
Proposition

4.7
would give the exponentially loose bound
\gamma_{H}\leq 2^{2N}\epsilon_{H}
. To resolve this problem, we observe that it suffices to consider

(\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{2N})\in\{z\in\{0,1\}^{2N}\mid\text{Hamming weight of }z\text{ is at most }1\}\cup\{1^{2N}\},

(4.87)

to prove
Proposition

4.22
. This is why we choose
\theta\in[2N]\cup\{0,\diamond\}
. Recall from
Fig.

2
that our
\theta=0
corresponds to
(\theta_{1},\dots,\theta_{2N})=0^{2N}
, our

\theta\in[2N]
corresponds to the string with
1
at index
\theta
and
0
everywhere else,
and our
\theta=\diamond
corresponds to
1^{2N}
.

Proposition 4.22
.

Let
D
be an efficient perfect device. For all
i,\theta\in[2N]
, we have

\{Z_{i},X_{i}\}\approx_{\sqrt{\gamma_{H}}+{\mathrm{negl}(\lambda)},\sigma^{\theta}}0.

(4.88)

We need a few lemmas first.

Lemma 4.23
.

Let
D
be an efficient device. For all
i,\theta\in[2N]
, we have

\sum_{a}\mathrm{Tr}[X_{i}Z_{i}^{(a)}\sigma^{\theta}Z_{i}^{(a)}]\approx_{\sqrt{\gamma_{H}}+{\mathrm{negl}(\lambda)}}0.

(4.89)

Proof.

The proof generalizes that of
[
MV21
, Lemma 4.18]
and is more refined due to the use of
\zeta
.

We prove the lemma for
i=1
as the proof is analogous for other
i
. Now, the states in
\{\sum_{a}Z_{1}^{(a)}\sigma^{\theta}Z_{1}^{(a)}\mid\theta\in[2N]\}
are computationally indistinguishable because they can be efficiently prepared from
\sigma^{\theta}
by measuring
Z_{1}
(
Z_{1}
is efficient because
D
is efficient) and the
\sigma^{\theta}
s are computationally indistinguishable (
Lemma

4.11
). In addition, because
D
is efficient,
X_{1}
is an efficient binary observable. Therefore, by part
1
of the lifting lemma (
Lemma

2.16
), it suffices to prove the current lemma for
\theta=2
.

For
a\in\{0,1\}
, we define

\displaystyle\sigma(a)\coloneqq\sum_{v|v_{1}=a}\sigma^{2,v}\quad\text{and}\quad\zeta(a)\coloneqq\sum_{v|v_{1}=a}\zeta(2,1,v).

(4.90)

Note that
\sigma^{2}=\sigma(0)+\sigma(1)
by definition and
\zeta(0)+\zeta(1)\leq 4\gamma_{H}
by
Lemma

4.10
.

By
Eq.

4.45
, we have

\zeta(a)/4=\mathrm{Tr}[\sigma(a)]-\mathrm{Tr}[Z_{1}^{(a)}\sigma(a)],

(4.91)

which can be re-expressed as

Z_{1}^{(a)}\approx_{\zeta(a),\sigma(a)}I,\quad Z_{1}^{(\overline{a})}\approx_{\zeta(a),\sigma(a)}0.

(4.92)

Therefore, by
Lemma

2.8
, we have

Z_{1}^{(a)}\sigma(a)Z_{1}^{(a)}\approx_{\zeta(a)}\sigma(a),\quad Z_{1}^{(\overline{a})}\sigma(a)Z_{1}^{(\overline{a})}\approx_{\zeta(a)}0.

(4.93)

Therefore, by the triangle inequality (recall that the definition of
\approx
for states involves a square), we have

\sum_{a,v}Z_{1}^{(a)}\sigma^{2,v}Z_{1}^{(a)}=\sum_{a}Z_{1}^{(a)}(\sigma(0)+\sigma(1))Z_{1}^{(a)}\approx_{\left(\sqrt{\zeta(0)}+\sqrt{\zeta(1)}\right)^{2}}\sigma^{2}.

(4.94)

Recalling
\zeta(0)+\zeta(1)\leq 4\gamma_{H}
by
Lemma

4.10
, the above equation means

\sum_{a,v}Z_{1}^{(a)}\sigma^{2,v}Z_{1}^{(a)}\approx_{\gamma_{H}}\sigma^{2}.

(4.95)

Finally, using the above equation and
Lemma

4.20
, we obtain

\sum_{a}\mathrm{Tr}[X_{1}Z_{1}^{(a)}\sigma^{2}Z_{1}^{(a)}]=\mathrm{Tr}\Bigl[X_{1}\sum_{a,v}Z_{1}^{(a)}\sigma^{2,v}Z_{1}^{(a)}\Bigr]\approx_{\sqrt{\gamma_{H}}}\mathrm{Tr}[X_{1}\sigma^{2}]\approx_{\gamma_{H}}0,

(4.96)

which completes the proof.
∎

To state and prove the next lemma, we need the following definition.

Definition 4.24
.

Let
D
be a device. For keys
k\in(\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{F}}\cup\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{G}})^{2N}
,
b\in\{0,1\}^{2N}
,
y\in\mathcal{Y}^{2N}\textbf{}
, we define
\hat{x}(b,k,y)\in\mathcal{X}^{2N}
component-wise by
\hat{x}(b,k,y)_{i}\coloneqq\hat{x}(b_{i},k_{i},y_{i})
for all
i\in[2N]
. Then, we define

\hat{\Pi}^{b}_{y}\coloneqq\Pi^{b,\hat{x}(b,k,y)}_{y}\quad\text{and}\quad\hat{\Pi}^{b}\coloneqq\sum_{y}\hat{\Pi}^{b}_{y}\otimes|y\rangle\langle y|,

(4.97)

which can be thought of as projectors onto the correct preimage answers.
Note that if
\hat{x}(b_{i},k_{i},y_{i})=\perp
for some
i\in[2N]
, then
\hat{\Pi}_{y}^{b}=0
.
Moreover, for
a\in\{0,1\}
and
i\in[2N]
, we define

\hat{\Pi}^{i,a}_{y}\coloneqq\sum_{b|b_{i}=a}\hat{\Pi}^{b}_{y},\quad\hat{\Pi}^{i,a}\coloneqq\sum_{y}\hat{\Pi}^{i,a}_{y}\otimes|y\rangle\langle y|,\quad\text{and}\quad Z^{(a)}_{i,d}\coloneqq\sum_{y}Z^{(a)}_{i,y,d}\otimes|y\rangle\langle y|.

(4.98)

We also note that, by previous definitions, we have

Z_{i,y,d}^{(a)}=\sum_{v\mid v_{i}=a}P_{0^{2N},y,d}^{v}\quad\text{and}\quad Z_{i}^{(a)}=\sum_{d}Z^{(a)}_{i,d}\otimes|d\rangle\langle d|.

(4.99)

Lemma 4.25
.

Let
D
be a device. Let
\theta\in[2N]\cup\{0\}

and
b,b^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}^{2N}
be such that
b_{j}\neq b^{\prime}_{j}

for some
j\in[2N]
with
j\neq\theta
. Then,
\hat{\Pi}^{b}_{y}\psi^{\theta}_{y}\hat{\Pi}^{b^{\prime}}_{y}=0
, for all
y\in\mathcal{Y}^{2N}
.

Proof.

This proof generalizes that of
[
MV21
, Lemma 4.20]
.
We consider the following three cases, which cover all possibilities:

1.

\hat{b}(k_{j},y_{j})=b_{j}
. In this case, we have
y_{j}\in\mathbin{\scalebox{1.3}{$\cup$}}_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\Supp(f_{k_{j},b_{j}}(x))
. Now,
k_{j}\in\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{G}}
as
j\neq\theta
. Therefore,
b_{j}^{\prime}\neq b_{j}
implies
y_{j}\notin\mathbin{\scalebox{1.3}{$\cup$}}_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\Supp(f_{k_{j},b_{j}^{\prime}}(x))
. So
\hat{x}(b_{j}^{\prime},k_{j},y_{j})=\perp
and
\hat{\Pi}^{b^{\prime}}_{y}=0
.

2.

\hat{b}(k_{j},y_{j})=b_{j}^{\prime}
. In this case, by an analogous argument, we obtain
\hat{\Pi}^{b}_{y}=0
.

3.

\hat{b}(k_{j},y_{j})=\perp
. In this case, we have
\hat{x}(b_{j},k_{j},y_{j})=\hat{x}(b_{j}^{\prime},k_{j},y_{j})=\perp
. So
\hat{\Pi}^{b}_{y}=\hat{\Pi}^{b^{\prime}}_{y}=0
.

In all cases, we see
\hat{\Pi}^{b}_{y}\psi^{\theta}_{y}\hat{\Pi}^{b^{\prime}}_{y}=0
. Hence the lemma.
∎

Lemma 4.26
.

Let
D
be an efficient perfect device. For all
i,\theta\in[2N]
, we have

\sum_{a,y,d}\|M_{y}^{d}\hat{\Pi}^{i,a}_{y}-Z^{(a)}_{i,y,d}M_{y}^{d}\|^{2}_{\psi^{\theta}_{y}}\simeq_{2\gamma_{H}+{\mathrm{negl}(\lambda)}}0.

(4.100)

Proof.

The proof of this lemma generalizes that of
[
MV21
, Lemma 4.19]
.

We prove the lemma for
i=1
as the proof for other
i
is analogous. Expanding the left-hand side of
Eq.

4.100
, we obtain

\displaystyle\sum_{a,y,d}\|M_{y}^{d}\hat{\Pi}^{1,a}_{y}-Z^{(a)}_{1,y,d}M_{y}^{d}\|^{2}_{\psi^{\theta}_{y}}

(4.101)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{a,y,d}\mathrm{Tr}[(\hat{\Pi}^{1,a}_{y}M_{y}^{d}-M_{y}^{d}Z^{(a)}_{1,y,d})(M_{y}^{d}\hat{\Pi}^{1,a}_{y}-Z^{(a)}_{1,y,d}M_{y}^{d})\psi^{\theta}_{y}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{a,y,d}\mathrm{Tr}[\hat{\Pi}^{1,a}_{y}M_{y}^{d}\hat{\Pi}^{1,a}_{y}\psi^{\theta}_{y}]+\sum_{a,y,d}\mathrm{Tr}[M_{y}^{d}Z^{(a)}_{1,y,d}M_{y}^{d}\psi^{\theta}_{y}]-\sum_{a,y,d}\mathrm{Tr}[M_{y}^{d}Z^{(a)}_{1,y,d}M_{y}^{d}(\hat{\Pi}^{1,a}_{y}\psi_{y}^{\theta}+\psi_{y}^{\theta}\hat{\Pi}^{1,a}_{y})].

Since
\psi^{\theta}=\sum_{y}\psi_{y}^{\theta}\otimes|y\rangle\langle y|
,

M^{d}=\sum_{y}M_{y}^{d}\otimes|y\rangle\langle y|
,

\hat{\Pi}^{a}_{i}=\sum_{y}\hat{\Pi}^{i,a}_{y}\otimes|y\rangle\langle y|

and

Z^{(a)}_{i,d}=\sum_{y}Z^{(a)}_{1,y,d}\otimes|y\rangle\langle y|
,
we can simplify the left-hand side of
Eq.

4.100

as

\sum_{a,d}\mathrm{Tr}[\hat{\Pi}^{1,a}M^{d}\hat{\Pi}^{1,a}\psi^{\theta}]+\sum_{a,d}\mathrm{Tr}[M^{d}Z^{(a)}_{1,d}M^{d}\psi^{\theta}]-\sum_{a,d}\mathrm{Tr}[Z^{(a)}_{1,d}M^{d}(\hat{\Pi}^{1,a}\psi^{\theta}+\psi^{\theta}\hat{\Pi}^{1,a})M^{d}].

(4.102)

Because the device is perfect, according to
4.6
, the first term is

\sum_{a,d}\mathrm{Tr}[\hat{\Pi}^{1,a}M^{d}\hat{\Pi}^{1,a}\psi^{\theta}]=\sum_{a}\mathrm{Tr}\Bigl[\hat{\Pi}^{1,a}\Bigl(\sum_{d}M^{d}\Bigr)\hat{\Pi}^{1,a}\psi^{\theta}\Bigr]=\sum_{a}\mathrm{Tr}[\hat{\Pi}^{1,a}\psi^{\theta}]=1-{\mathrm{negl}(\lambda)}.

(4.103)

The second term is

\sum_{d}\mathrm{Tr}\Bigl[M^{d}\Bigl(\sum_{a}Z^{(a)}_{1,d}\Bigr)M^{d}\psi^{\theta}\Bigr]=1.

(4.104)

For the third term, we first notice that

\lVert\hat{\Pi}^{1,0}+\hat{\Pi}^{1,1}-\mathbbm{1}\rVert^{2}_{\psi^{\theta}}=1-\mathrm{Tr}[(\hat{\Pi}^{1,0}+\hat{\Pi}^{1,1})\psi^{\theta}]\leq{\mathrm{negl}(\lambda)},

(4.105)

that is

\hat{\Pi}^{1,0}+\hat{\Pi}^{1,1}\simeq_{{\mathrm{negl}(\lambda)},\psi^{\theta}}\mathbbm{1}.

(4.106)

Then, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{a}\mathrm{Tr}\Bigl[\Bigl(\sum_{d}M^{d}Z^{(a)}_{1,d}M^{d}\Bigr)(\hat{\Pi}^{1,a}\psi^{\theta}+\psi^{\theta}\hat{\Pi}^{1,a})\Bigr]

(4.107)

\displaystyle\simeq_{{\mathrm{negl}(\lambda)}}\sum_{a,d}\mathrm{Tr}[M^{d}Z^{(a)}_{1,d}M^{d}(\hat{\Pi}^{1,a}\psi^{\theta}(\hat{\Pi}^{0}_{1}+\hat{\Pi}^{1}_{1})+(\hat{\Pi}^{0}_{1}+\hat{\Pi}^{1}_{1})\psi^{\theta}\hat{\Pi}^{1,a})M^{d}]

\displaystyle=2\sum_{a,d}\mathrm{Tr}[Z^{(a)}_{1,d}M^{d}\hat{\Pi}^{1,a}\psi^{\theta}\hat{\Pi}^{1,a}M^{d}]+\sum_{a,d}\mathrm{Tr}[Z^{(a)}_{1,d}M^{d}(\hat{\Pi}^{1,a}\psi^{\theta}\hat{\Pi}^{1,1-a}+\hat{\Pi}^{1,1-a}\psi^{\theta}\hat{\Pi}^{1,a})M^{d}],

where the first approximation follows from the replacement lemma (
Lemma

2.6
) with
Eq.

4.106
,
\|\hat{\Pi}^{1,a}\|_{\infty}\leq 1
, and
\lVert\sum_{d}M^{d}Z_{1,d}^{(a)}M^{d}\rVert_{\infty}\leq 1
. The last bound follows from
0\leq\sum_{d}M^{d}Z_{1,d}^{(a)}M^{d}\leq\sum_{d}M^{d}=\mathbbm{1}
which is implied by
0\leq Z_{1,d}^{(a)}\leq\mathbbm{1}
.

Now, the second term appearing on the last line
Eq.

4.107
is zero, which follows from:
\hat{\Pi}^{1,a}\psi^{\theta}\hat{\Pi}^{1,1-a}+\hat{\Pi}^{1,1-a}\psi^{\theta}\hat{\Pi}^{1,a}
is independent of
a
,
\sum_{a}Z_{1,d}^{(a)}=\mathbbm{1}
,
\sum_{d}M^{d}=\mathbbm{1}
, and
\hat{\Pi}^{1,0}\,\hat{\Pi}^{1,1}=0
.

We argue that the first term appearing on the last line
Eq.

4.107
is close to
2
. For
\theta=0
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{d}M^{d}\hat{\Pi}^{1,a}\psi^{0}\hat{\Pi}^{1,a}M_{y}^{d}\otimes|d\rangle\langle d|

\displaystyle=\sum_{y}\sum_{d}\,\sum_{b,b^{\prime}\mid b_{1}=b_{1}^{\prime}=a}M_{y}^{d}\,\hat{\Pi}^{b}_{y}\psi_{y}^{0}\hat{\Pi}^{b^{\prime}}_{y}\,M^{d}\otimes|y,d\rangle\langle y,d|

(4.108)

\displaystyle=\sum_{y}\sum_{d}\sum_{b\mid b_{1}=a}M_{y}^{d}\,\hat{\Pi}^{b}_{y}\psi_{y}^{0}\hat{\Pi}^{b}_{y}\,M_{y}^{d}\otimes|y,d\rangle\langle y,d|

(4.109)

\displaystyle\simeq_{{\mathrm{negl}(\lambda)}}\sum_{\text{valid }y\mid\hat{b}(k_{1},y_{1})=a}\,\sum_{d}M_{y}^{d}\,\hat{\Pi}^{\hat{b}(k,y)}_{y}\psi_{y}^{0}\hat{\Pi}^{\hat{b}(k,y)}_{y}\,M_{y}^{d}\otimes|y,d\rangle\langle y,d|

(4.110)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\text{valid }y\mid\hat{b}(k_{1},y_{1})=a}\,\sum_{d}M_{y}^{d}\psi_{y}^{0}M_{y}^{d}\otimes|y,d\rangle\langle y,d|=\sum_{v\mid v_{1}=a}\sigma^{0,v},

(4.111)

where we used
Lemma

4.25
in the second equality, and
\theta=0
(so
k\in\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{G}}^{2N}
) and the perfectness of
D
in the third (approximate) equality.

Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{a,d}\mathrm{Tr}[Z^{(a)}_{1,d}M^{d}\hat{\Pi}^{1,a}\psi^{0}\hat{\Pi}^{1,a}M^{d}]

\displaystyle=\sum_{a}\mathrm{Tr}[Z_{1}^{(a)}\sum_{d}M^{d}\hat{\Pi}^{1,a}\psi^{0}\hat{\Pi}^{1,a}M_{y}^{d}\otimes|d\rangle\langle d|]

(4.112)

\displaystyle\simeq_{{\mathrm{negl}(\lambda)}}\sum_{v}\mathrm{Tr}[Z_{1}^{(v_{1})}\sigma^{0,v}]\geq 1-\gamma_{H},

where the last inequality follows from
Lemma

4.9
.

But given any state
\psi^{\theta}
of
D
,
\sum_{a,d}\mathrm{Tr}[Z^{(a)}_{1,d}M^{d}\hat{\Pi}^{1,a}\psi^{\theta}\hat{\Pi}^{1,a}M^{d}]
can be estimated by the following efficient algorithm that is independent of
\theta
:

1.

Measure
\psi^{\theta}
using the efficient measurement
\{\sum_{b_{2}\ldots b_{2N},x}\Pi^{b_{1}b_{2}\ldots b_{2N},x}\}_{b_{1}}
to obtain outcome
b_{1}\in\{0,1\}
.

2.

Measure
M^{d}
to obtain outcome
d\in\{0,1\}^{2Nw}
.

3.

Measure
Z_{1,d}
to obtain bit
b_{1}^{\prime}
.

4.

Output
1
if
b_{1}=b_{1}^{\prime}
and
0
otherwise.

Because
D
is perfect, the state of the system after the first step is negligibly close to
\sum_{a}\hat{\Pi}^{1,a}\psi^{\theta}\hat{\Pi}^{1,a}
in trace distance. After the second step, the state becomes
\sum_{a,d}M^{d}\hat{\Pi}^{1,a}\psi^{\theta}\hat{\Pi}^{1,a}M^{d}
. Therefore, the probability the algorithm outputs
1
is negligibly close to
\sum_{a,d}\mathrm{Tr}[Z^{(a)}_{1,d}M^{d}\hat{\Pi}^{1,a}\psi^{\theta}\hat{\Pi}^{1,a}M^{d}]
.

Therefore,
Eq.

4.112
and the computational indistinguishability of the
\psi^{\theta}
s (
Lemma

4.11
) gives

\sum_{a,d}\mathrm{Tr}[Z^{(a)}_{1,d}M^{d}\hat{\Pi}^{1,a}\psi^{\theta}\hat{\Pi}^{1,a}M^{d}]\geq 1-\gamma_{H}+{\mathrm{negl}(\lambda)}.

(4.113)

Overall, putting together the bounds we have derived for each of the three terms in
Eq.

4.102
, we obtain

\displaystyle\sum_{a,y,d}\|M_{y}^{d}\hat{\Pi}_{i,y}^{a}-Z^{(a)}_{i,y,d}M_{y}^{d}\|^{2}_{\psi^{\theta}_{y}}\leq 2\gamma_{H}+{\mathrm{negl}(\lambda)},

(4.114)

which completes the proof.
∎

Lemma 4.27
.

Let
D
be an efficient perfect device. For all
\theta\in[2N]
and
c\in\{0,1\}
, we have

\sum_{a,v|v_{\theta}=c}\mathrm{Tr}[X_{\theta}Z_{\theta}^{(a)}\sigma^{\theta,v}Z_{\theta}^{(a)}]\approx_{\sqrt{\gamma_{H}}+{\mathrm{negl}(\lambda)}}0.

(4.115)

Proof.

The proof of this lemma generalizes that of
[
MV21
, Lemma 4.18]
.

It suffices to prove the lemma for
\theta=1
as the argument is analogous for other
\theta
. Define

E_{c}\coloneqq\sum_{a,v|v_{1}=c}\mathrm{Tr}[X_{1}Z_{1}^{(a)}\sigma^{1,v}Z_{1}^{(a)}].

(4.116)

By
Lemma

4.23
, we have
E_{0}+E_{1}\approx_{\sqrt{\gamma_{H}}+{\mathrm{negl}(\lambda)}}0
. Therefore, it suffices to show that
E_{0}\approx_{\sqrt{\gamma_{H}}+{\mathrm{negl}(\lambda)}}E_{1}
. Using the definitions of
\sigma^{1,v}
and
\Sigma(1,v)
from
4.4
, and using
4.3
, we have

\displaystyle E_{c}

\displaystyle=\sum_{a,v|v_{1}=c}\,\sum_{y,d\in\Sigma_{1,v}}\mathrm{Tr}[X_{1}Z_{1}^{(a)}M_{y}^{d}\,\psi_{y}^{1}\,M_{y}^{d}\otimes|y,d\rangle\langle y,d|Z_{1}^{(a)}]

(4.117)

\displaystyle=\sum_{a,v|v_{1}=c}\,\sum_{y,d\in\mathrm{\Sigma}_{1,v}}\mathrm{Tr}[X_{1,y,d}Z_{1,y,d}^{(a)}M_{y}^{d}\,\psi_{y}^{1}\,M_{y}^{d}Z_{1,y,d}^{(a)}]

\displaystyle=\sum_{a,y}\,\sum_{d|\hat{h}(k_{1},y_{1},d_{1})=c}\mathrm{Tr}[X_{1,y,d}Z_{1,y,d}^{(a)}M_{y}^{d}\,\psi_{y}^{1}\,M_{y}^{d}Z_{1,y,d}^{(a)}].

Now, also define

F_{c}\coloneqq\sum_{a,y}\,\sum_{d|\hat{h}(k_{1},y_{1},d_{1})=c}\mathrm{Tr}[X_{1,y,d}M^{d}_{y}\hat{\Pi}^{1,a}_{y}\psi^{1}_{y}\hat{\Pi}^{1,a}_{y}M^{d}_{y}].

(4.118)

By the technical
4.28
, deferred to after this proof, we have
E_{c}\approx_{\sqrt{\gamma_{H}}}F_{c}
. Therefore, to prove the lemma, it suffices to prove
F_{0}\simeq_{{\mathrm{negl}(\lambda)}}F_{1}
. To this end, we first substitute the definition
\hat{\Pi}^{1,a}_{y}\coloneqq\sum_{b|b_{1}=a}\hat{\Pi}^{b}_{y}
into the expression for
F_{c}
:

F_{c}=\sum_{b,y}\,\sum_{d|\hat{h}(k_{1},y_{1},d_{1})=c}\mathrm{Tr}[X_{1,y,d}M^{d}_{y}\hat{\Pi}^{b}_{y}\psi^{1}_{y}\hat{\Pi}^{b}_{y}M^{d}_{y}]+\sum_{a,y}\,\sum_{d|\hat{h}(k_{1},y_{1},d_{1})=c}\,\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}b\neq b^{\prime}\\
b_{1}=b_{1}^{\prime}=a\end{subarray}}\mathrm{Tr}[X_{1,y,d}M_{y}^{d}\hat{\Pi}_{y}^{b}\psi^{1}_{y}\hat{\Pi}^{b^{\prime}}_{y}M_{y}^{d}].

(4.119)

But by
Lemma

4.25
, the second term is
0
. Therefore,

F_{c}=\sum_{b,y}\,\sum_{d|\hat{h}(k_{1},y_{1},d_{1})=c}\mathrm{Tr}[X_{1,y,d}M^{d}_{y}\hat{\Pi}^{b}_{y}\psi^{1}_{y}\hat{\Pi}^{b}_{y}M^{d}_{y}].

(4.120)

With the above expression in hand, we prove
F_{0}\simeq_{{\mathrm{negl}(\lambda)}}F_{1}
by contradiction. Assume that there exists a non-negligible function
\mu
such that
F_{0}-F_{1}>{\mu(\lambda)}>0
for some
\lambda
(as in the proof of
Lemma

4.19
, the case
F_{1}-F_{0}>{\mu(\lambda)}>0
can be handled analogously). We can then construct the following efficient algorithm
\mathcal{A}
which breaks the adaptive hardcore bit property of ENTCFs (
(d)
).

1.

Input: a key
k_{1}
sampled from
\mathrm{Gen}_{\mathcal{F}}(1^{\lambda})_{\mathrm{key}}
.

2.

Independently sample
2N-1
keys
k_{2},\ldots,k_{2N-1}
, each from
\mathrm{Gen}_{\mathcal{G}}(1^{\lambda})_{\mathrm{key}}
.

3.

Prepare the state
\psi^{1}
, then make measurement obtaining
y
, then make preimage measurement obtaining
(b,x)
, then make Hadamard measurement obtaining
d
, and then measure
X_{1}
obtaining (single-bit)
h
.

4.

Output:
(y_{1},b_{1},x_{1},d_{1},h)
and print “
f_{k_{1},b_{1}}(x_{1})=y_{1}\text{ and }\hat{h}(k_{1},y_{1},d_{1})=h
”.

Because
D
is perfect, we have, with probability
\geq 1-{\mathrm{negl}(\lambda)}
,

f_{k_{1},b_{1}}(x_{1})=y_{1}.

(4.121)

Write
\hat{h}
as shorthand for
\hat{h}(k_{1},y_{1},d_{1})
. Using
F_{c}=\mathrm{Pr}_{\mathcal{A}}(h=0,\hat{h}=c)-\mathrm{Pr}_{\mathcal{A}}(h=1,\hat{h}=c)
, we find

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\hat{h}=h)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Pr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\hat{h}=h=0)+\mathrm{Pr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\hat{h}=h=1)

(4.122)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Pr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\hat{h}=0,h=1)+\mathrm{Pr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\hat{h}=1,h=0)+F_{0}-F_{1}

\displaystyle>\mathrm{Pr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\hat{h}\neq h)+{\mu(\lambda)}.

Eqs.

4.121
and
4.122
contradict the adaptive hardcore bit property of ENTCFs (
(d)
) as required.
∎

Claim 4.28
.

Under the same setup as in the proof of
Lemma

4.27
, for any
c\in\{0,1\}
, we have

\displaystyle E_{c}

\displaystyle\approx_{\sqrt{\gamma_{H}}}F_{c}.

(4.123)

Proof.

The proof of this claim generalizes that of
[
MV21
, Lemma 4.22]
.

By simple algebra, we have

\displaystyle E_{c}-F_{c}

\displaystyle=\sum_{a,y}\,\sum_{d|\hat{h}(k_{1},y_{1},d_{1})=c}\langle X_{1,y,d}Z^{(a)}_{1,y,d}M_{y}^{d},Z^{(a)}_{1,y,d}M_{y}^{d}-M_{y}^{d}\hat{\Pi}^{1,a}_{y}\rangle_{\psi^{1}_{y}}

(4.124)

\displaystyle+\langle Z^{(a)}_{1,y,d}M_{y}^{d}-M_{y}^{d}\hat{\Pi}^{1,a}_{y},X_{1,y,d}M_{y}^{d}\hat{\Pi}^{1,a}_{y}\rangle_{\psi^{1}_{y}}.

Therefore, by Cauchy-Schwarz, we have

\displaystyle\lvert E_{c}-F_{c}\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{a,y,d}\|X_{1,y,d}Z^{(a)}_{1,y,d}M_{y}^{d}\|_{\psi^{1}_{y}}\cdot\|Z^{(a)}_{1,y,d}M_{y}^{d}-M_{y}^{d}\hat{\Pi}^{1,a}_{y}\|_{\psi^{1}_{y}}

(4.125)

\displaystyle+\|Z^{(a)}_{1,y,d}M_{y}^{d}-M_{y}^{d}\hat{\Pi}^{1,a}_{y}\|_{\psi^{1}_{y}}\cdot\|X_{1,y,d}M_{y}^{d}\hat{\Pi}^{1,a}_{y}\|_{{\psi^{1}_{y}}},

where we dropped the restriction on
d
, which only makes the upper bound looser.

Again by Cauchy-Schwarz, we have

\displaystyle\lvert E_{c}-F_{c}\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\sum_{a,y,d}\|X_{1,y,d}Z^{(a)}_{1,y,d}M_{y}^{d}\|_{\psi^{1}_{y}}^{2}}\cdot\sqrt{\sum_{a,y,d}\|Z^{(a)}_{1,y,d}M_{y}^{d}-M_{y}^{d}\hat{\Pi}^{1,a}_{y}\|_{\psi^{1}_{y}}^{2}}

(4.126)

\displaystyle+\sqrt{\sum_{a,y,d}\|Z^{(a)}_{1,y,d}M_{y}^{d}-M_{y}^{d}\hat{\Pi}^{1,a}_{y}\|_{\psi^{1}_{y}}^{2}}\cdot\sqrt{\sum_{a,y,d}\|X_{1,y,d}M_{y}^{d}\hat{\Pi}^{1,a}_{y}\|_{\psi^{1}_{y}}^{2}}.

Now, we have

\sum_{a,y,d}\|X_{1,y,d}Z^{(a)}_{1,y,d}M_{y}^{d}\|_{\psi^{1}_{y}}^{2}=1
and

\sum_{a,y,d}\|X_{1,y,d}M_{y}^{d}\hat{\Pi}^{1,a}_{y}\|_{{\psi^{1}_{y}}}\simeq_{{\mathrm{negl}(\lambda)}}1
, where the first equality directly follows from definitions and the second equality follows as
D
is perfect.

Hence,

|E_{c}-F_{c}|\leq 2\sqrt{\sum_{a,y,d}\|Z^{(a)}_{1,y,d}M_{y}^{d}-M_{y}^{d}\hat{\Pi}^{1,a}_{y}\|_{\psi^{1}_{y}}}\leq 2\sqrt{\gamma_{H}},

(4.127)

where the last inequality follows from

Lemma

4.26
.
∎

Using the last lemma,
Lemma

4.27
, we can now prove
Proposition

4.22
as follows.

Proof of
Proposition

4.22
.

The proof of this proposition generalizes that of
[
MV21
, Proposition 4.17]
.

Because
D
is efficient,
X_{i}
and
Z_{i}
are efficient binary observables. Therefore, it suffices to prove the proposition for
\theta=i
by the computational indistinguishability of the
\sigma^{\theta}
s (
Lemma

4.11
) and part
4
of the lifting lemma (
Lemma

2.16
). Moreover, it suffices to only prove the proposition for
\theta=i=1
as the argument is analogous otherwise.

\displaystyle\mathrm{Tr}[\{Z_{1},X_{1}\}^{2}\sigma^{1}]

\displaystyle=4\sum_{v}\mathrm{Tr}[(X_{1}Z_{1}^{(0)}X_{1}Z_{1}^{(0)}+Z_{1}^{(1)}X_{1}Z_{1}^{(1)}X_{1}]\sigma^{1,v})

(4.128)

\displaystyle=4\sum_{v}(-1)^{v_{1}}\mathrm{Tr}[(Z_{1}^{(0)}X_{1}Z_{1}^{(0)}+Z_{1}^{(1)}X_{1}Z_{1}^{(1)}]\sigma^{1,v})+O(\sqrt{\chi(1,v)}\cdot\sqrt{\mathrm{Tr}[\sigma^{1,v}]})

\displaystyle\approx_{\sqrt{\gamma_{H}}+{\mathrm{negl}(\lambda)}}0+O\Bigr(\sum_{v}\sqrt{\chi(1,v)}\cdot\sqrt{\mathrm{Tr}[\sigma^{1,v}]}\Bigr),

where the first equality is by simple algebra, the second equality is by
4.8
and the replacement lemma (note that
\|Z_{1}^{(a)}X_{1}Z_{1}^{(a)}\|_{\infty}\leq 1
for
a\in\{0,1\}
), and the last approximation is by
Lemma

4.27
with
\theta=1
(which uses the perfectness of
D
). But, by Cauchy-Schwarz, we have

\sum_{v}\sqrt{\chi(1,v)}\cdot\sqrt{\mathrm{Tr}[\sigma^{1,v}]}\leq\sqrt{\Bigl(\sum_{v}\chi(1,v)\Bigr)\Bigl(\sum_{v}\mathrm{Tr}[\sigma^{1,v}]\Bigr)}\leq\sqrt{\gamma_{H}}.

(4.129)

Therefore,
Eq.

4.128
implies that

\mathrm{Tr}[\{Z_{1},X_{1}\}^{2}\sigma^{1}]\approx_{\sqrt{\gamma_{H}}+{\mathrm{negl}(\lambda)}}0,

(4.130)

which completes the proof.
∎

4.5
Operator-state commutation

In this subsection, we prove
Proposition

4.29
that shows the
\sigma^{\theta}
states approximately commute with
Z_{i}
and
X_{i}
for certain pairs of
(\theta,i)
.

Proposition 4.29

(Operator-state commutation)
.

Let
D
be an efficient perfect device. For all
i,\theta\in[2N]
with
i\neq\theta
, we have

Z_{i}\,\sigma^{\theta}\approx_{\gamma_{H}+{\mathrm{negl}(\lambda)}}\sigma^{\theta}\,Z_{i}\quad\text{and}\quad X_{\theta}\,\sigma^{\theta}\approx_{\gamma_{H}+{\mathrm{negl}(\lambda)}}\sigma^{\theta}\,X_{\theta},

(4.131)

moreover, for all
q\in\{0,1\}^{2N}
, we have

\displaystyle Z_{q,i}\,\sigma^{\theta}

\displaystyle\,\approx_{\gamma_{H}+\gamma_{H,q}+{\mathrm{negl}(\lambda)}}\sigma^{\theta}\,Z_{q,i}

\displaystyle\text{if $q_{i}=0$},

(4.132)

\displaystyle X_{q,\theta}\,\sigma^{\theta}

\displaystyle\,\approx_{\gamma_{H}+\gamma_{H,q}+{\mathrm{negl}(\lambda)}}\sigma^{\theta}\,X_{q,\theta}

\displaystyle\text{if $q_{\theta}=1$}.

Proof.

The equations in
Eq.

4.132
follow from those in
Eq.

4.131
by
Proposition

4.14
and
Lemma

2.8
. The proofs of the two equations in
Eq.

4.131
are analogous. We prove the first in detail and comment on the minor changes required to prove the second.

To prove the first equation, it suffices to prove it for
i=1
and
\theta=2
as the proof for other
i\neq\theta
is analogous. By the triangle inequality and
Lemma

4.17
, we have

\|Z_{1}\sigma^{2}Z_{1}-\sigma^{2}\|_{1}\leq\sum_{v}\|Z_{1}\sigma^{2,v}Z_{1}-\sigma^{2,v}\|_{1}+{\mathrm{negl}(\lambda)}.

(4.133)

We bound each term in the sum. Recall the definition
\zeta(2,1,v)\coloneqq\|Z_{1}-(-1)^{v_{1}}\mathbbm{1}\|_{\sigma^{2},v}^{2}
from
4.8
. Therefore, by
Lemma

2.8
, we have

\|Z_{1}\sigma^{2,v}Z_{1}-\sigma^{2,v}\|_{1}=\|Z_{1}\sigma^{2,v}Z_{1}-(-1)^{v_{1}}\mathbbm{1}\sigma^{2,v}(-1)^{v_{1}}\mathbbm{1}\|_{1}\leq 2\sqrt{\zeta(2,1,v)}\cdot\sqrt{\mathrm{Tr}[\sigma^{2,v}]}.

(4.134)

Therefore, resuming
Eq.

4.133
:

\|Z_{1}\sigma^{2}Z_{1}-\sigma^{2}\|_{1}\leq 2\sum_{v}\sqrt{\zeta(2,1,v)}\cdot\sqrt{\mathrm{Tr}[\sigma^{2,v}]}+{\mathrm{negl}(\lambda)}\leq 4\sqrt{\gamma_{H}}+{\mathrm{negl}(\lambda)},

(4.135)

where the last inequality follows from Cauchy-Schwarz and bounds on
\zeta
(and
\chi
) in
Lemma

4.10
. Hence we have proved the first equation.

To prove the second equation in
Eq.

4.131
, it suffices to prove it for
\theta=1
. Then, we can exactly reuse the above proof after changing the symbols
\{Z_{1},\sigma^{2},\sigma^{2,v},\zeta(2,1,v)\}
to
\{X_{1},\sigma^{1},\sigma^{1,v},\chi(1,v)\}
, respectively.
∎

Observe that
Proposition

4.29
does
not
say
Z_{q,i}
and
X_{q,i}
commute with
\sigma^{\theta}

for all pairs
(i,\theta)
. To get around this problem, we use the computational indistinguishability of the
\sigma^{\theta}
s to argue that efficient observables must act similarly on different
\sigma^{\theta}
s. For example,

Z_{1}X_{3}Z_{2}\sigma^{2}\approx X_{3}Z_{1}Z_{2}\sigma^{2},

(4.136)

does not follow from
Proposition

4.29
, since
Z_{2}
does not commute with
\sigma^{2}
. Nevertheless, by using the computational indistinguishability of
\sigma^{2}
and
\sigma^{3}
, we can derive an “operational version” of
Eq.

4.136
. The operational version allows us to interchange the left-hand and right-hand sides of
Eq.

4.136
when they appear inside traces (i.e.,
\mathrm{Tr}
). We can only derive such an operational version because the computational indistinguishability of
\sigma^{2}
and
\sigma^{3}
only allows us to interchange
\sigma^{2}
and
\sigma^{3}
inside traces, see
Lemmas

2.16
and
2.17
. For a concrete example of how to use
Proposition

4.29
, see the long aligned equation in the proof of
Lemma

4.33
.

4.6
The swap isometry

In this subsection, we define the swap isometry that we will show maps the observables and states of the device onto the ideal ones.

Definition 4.30
.

Let
D
be a device and let
\mathcal{H}\coloneqq\mathcal{H}_{D}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{Y}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{R}
. The swap isometry is the map
\mathcal{V}:\mathcal{H}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{2^{2N}}\otimes\mathcal{H}
defined by

\mathcal{V}=\sum_{u\in\{0,1\}^{2N}}\ket{u}\otimes\prod_{i\in[2N]}X_{i}^{u_{i}}\prod_{j\in[2N]}Z_{j}^{(u_{j})}.

(4.137)

Note that by the commutation relations (
Proposition

4.21
) the ordering within each
\prod_{i\in[2N]}
does not matter.

Lemma 4.31
.

Let
D
be an efficient device. Then,
\mathcal{V}
is efficient.

Proof.

\mathcal{V}
can be implemented efficiently by the following circuit:

1.

Initialize
2N
ancilla qubits to
\ket{0}
.

2.

Apply
H^{\otimes 2N}
on the ancilla.

3.

Apply
2N
c-
Z_{i}
gates where the control is by the
i
-th ancilla qubit for
i=1,\ldots,2N
.

4.

Apply
H^{\otimes 2N}
on the ancilla.

5.

Apply
2N
c-
X_{i}
gates where the control is by the
i
-th ancilla qubit for
i=1,\ldots,2N
.

It can be verified by direct calculation that the above circuit indeed gives
\mathcal{V}
.

We illustrate
\mathcal{V}
when
2N=4
in
Fig.

3
below.

\ket{0}
\ket{0}
\ket{0}
\ket{0}
\ket{\psi}
H
H
H
H
Z_{1}
Z_{2}
Z_{3}
Z_{4}
H
H
H
H
X_{1}
X_{2}
X_{3}
X_{4}

Figure 3:
Illustration of the swap isometry
\mathcal{V}
when
2N=4
.

∎

4.7
Observables under the swap isometry

To prove the results in this and the following subsections, we make extensive use of
Proposition

4.29
.

Lemma 4.32
.

Let
D
be an efficient perfect device. For all
k\in[2N]
and
\theta\in[2N]\cup\{0,\diamond\}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}^{\dagger}(\sigma^{Z}_{k}\otimes\mathbbm{1})\mathcal{V}

\displaystyle=Z_{k},

(4.138)

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}^{\dagger}(\sigma^{X}_{k}\otimes\mathbbm{1})\mathcal{V}

\displaystyle\approx_{N\sqrt{\gamma_{H}},\sigma^{\theta}}X_{k},

moreover, for all
q\in\{0,1\}^{2N}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}^{\dagger}(\sigma^{Z}_{k}\otimes\mathbbm{1})\mathcal{V}

\displaystyle\approx_{\gamma_{H}+\gamma_{H,q}+{\mathrm{negl}(\lambda)},\sigma^{\theta}}Z_{q,k}

if
q_{k}=0
,

(4.139)

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}^{\dagger}(\sigma^{X}_{k}\otimes\mathbbm{1})\mathcal{V}

\displaystyle\approx_{N\sqrt{\gamma_{H}}+\gamma_{H,q},\sigma^{\theta}}X_{q,k}

if
q_{k}=1
.

(4.140)

Proof.

The last two equations follow immediately from the first two and
Proposition

4.14
(note
\gamma_{P}={\mathrm{negl}(\lambda)}
as
D
is perfect). The first equation follows by direct calculation using the fact that, for all
i\in[2N]
, we have
X_{i}^{2}=\mathbbm{1}
and
\sum_{a\in\{0,1\}}Z_{i}^{(a)}=\mathbbm{1}
.

Consider the second equation. For notational convenience, we write for
i\in[2N]
,
k\in[2N]
, and
u=(u_{i},\ldots,u_{2N})\in\{0,1\}^{2N-i+1}
,

1.

\mathcal{Z}_{i}(u)
for the product of the
2N
-th (from the left) operator down to the
i
th operator in the
2N
-tuple
(Z_{1}^{(u_{1})},Z_{2}^{(u_{2})},\ldots,Z_{k}^{(u_{k})},\ldots,Z_{2N}^{(u_{2N})})
. More precisely

\mathcal{Z}_{i}(u)\coloneqq Z_{2N}^{(u_{2N})}Z_{2N-1}^{(u_{2N-1})}\cdots Z_{i}^{(u_{i})}.

(4.141)

2.

\mathcal{Z}_{k,i}(u)
for the product of the
2N
-th operator down to the
i
th operator in the
2N
-tuple
